SOURCE PMC13664187.xml

SECTION 2.2. Development Cohort Construction and Target Panel Definition The analysis-ready 2D resource comprised 852 pan-cancer models with gene-dependency outcomes available for a fixed 20-gene target panel. Each model was identified by ModelID. To prevent information leakage from biologically related models, we used PatientID as the grouping variable; when PatientID was unavailable, we used ModelID as the fallback group identifier. The patient-grouped partition and fixed target panel are detailed in  Table S1 . Using a locked 20% test fraction and global random seed 20260826, we divided the 852 models into 681 development models and 171 locked 2D holdout models, representing 658 and 165 unique patient groups, respectively. No PatientID overlapped between partitions. We used the development partition to compare candidate input representations, learn target-specific features, and select hyperparameters. Within the current model-development workflow, holdout outcomes were not accessed until the development decisions and prediction files had been locked. This restriction did not apply retrospectively to the earlier target-panel selection. The response matrix comprised continuous CRISPR Gene Effect values for a 20-gene panel established before the present omics-candidate comparison. The panel included  CDK6 ,  STK11 ,  CCND3 ,  YAP1 ,  MDM4 ,  EP300 ,  CTNNB1 ,  EGFR ,  TSC2 ,  TSC1 ,  IGF1R ,  FGFR1 ,  CCNE1 ,  E2F3 ,  ERBB3 ,  NFE2L2 ,  BRAF ,  KEAP1 ,  MAPK1 , and  RAF1 . No target was added to or removed from the panel during the current omics ablation, the Broad and Sanger 3D evaluations, or the Tiriac pancreatic organoid projection. As documented in  Table S1 , the panel had been defined in an earlier 2D workflow that used all 852 models. Consequently, the 171-model holdout was independent of the current candidate representation, feature selection, hyperparameters, and model-fitting decisions, but not fully independent of historical target panel selection. Its performance was therefore interpreted as internal confirmation conditional on the selected panel, not an unbiased estimate of generalization for the complete analysis pipeline. Independent 3D validation assessed new samples for the same preselected genes; it did not evaluate transferability to unselected genes or across the genome. After the one-time holdout evaluation, we refitted the selected representation on all 852 models for downstream 3D deployment while retaining the fixed targets, target-specific feature identities, hyperparameters, and primary metric. Only preprocessing parameters and Elastic Net coefficients were re-estimated, as described in  Section 2.5 .

SECTION 2.3. Omics Data Preprocessing and Candidate Input Representations The candidate omics blocks were derived from processed molecular data in DepMap Public 26Q1 and aligned to the 852 models defined in  Section 2.2  [ 3 , 13 , 14 ]. All matrices were ordered by ModelID and verified against the training registry. Gene-level features were tracked using Entrez Gene identifiers. For each target, the response variable was the continuous CRISPR Gene Effect value; no target-dependency outcome was included in any predictor matrix. The gene-expression block comprised 19,215 protein-coding genes, represented as log2-transformed transcripts per million (TPM) values [log2(TPM + 1)], with no subsequent logarithmic transformation. The copy-number block comprised 19,955 whole-genome sequencing-derived gene-level linear, ploidy-relative values. Somatic variants were encoded separately as binary indicators of damage-mutation and hotspot-mutation carriers for 19,626 genes. OncotreeLineage was encoded as 29 binary indicators. The source blocks, encodings, initial dimensions, and candidate representations are summarized in  Table S2 . As shown in  Table S2 , lineage-only used the 29 lineage indicators as a non-molecular reference. Four molecular candidates were ordered by increasing representational complexity: expression-only (19,215 initial features); expression + copy number (39,170); expression + mutation (58,467), combining expression with damaging and hotspot indicators; and all omics + lineage (78,451), combining all four molecular blocks with lineage. The lineage-only reference was not eligible under the molecular one-standard-error selection rule. Eligibility screening was relearned within every applicable training partition. Expression and copy-number features were excluded when the missing-value fraction in the training partition exceeded 0.20 or the variance was less than or equal to 1 × 10 −8 . The same variance threshold was applied to mutation indicators and to lineage indicators within the all-omics candidate set; all 29 indicators were retained for the lineage-only reference. For each target, nonfinite values were temporarily replaced with the training-partition means solely for univariate screening; the 300 eligible features with the largest absolute Pearson correlations were then retained. For model fitting, missing values among the selected features were imputed using training-partition medians and standardized using means and standard deviations learned from the same training partition. These parameters were transferred unchanged to the corresponding validation or test observations. Filtering, screening, imputation, and standardization were repeated within each inner-training fold, each outer-training partition, and the 681-model development fit used for locked-holdout prediction. The 171 locked holdout models and all 3D data were excluded from learning feature eligibility, feature identities, imputation values, and scaling parameters.

SECTION 2.7. Sanger-148 Cohort Construction and Blinded Independent External Validation The independent Sanger 3D validation cohort was constructed from the RNA-seq and CRISPR-screening records of the tumor-derived organoid dependency map [ 39 ]. Before any Sanger target outcome was accessed, 162 organoids from 157 unique donors with both RNA-seq profiles and quality-controlled CRISPR-screening records were screened for eligibility. To prevent reuse of samples represented in the Broad exploratory layer or elsewhere in the Broad NextGen resource, Sanger sample and donor identifiers were cross-referenced against the Broad mapping records and the Broad-94 registry [ 38 , 39 ]. Thirteen Broad-linked organoids and one additional organoid from the same donor as a Broad-linked model were conservatively excluded. The final cohort therefore comprised 148 organoids from 144 unique donors, with no exact Broad-94 identity or identifiable same-donor Broad overlap. Cohort construction, harmonization, and the outcome-blinded lock procedure are summarized in  Table S6 . The final cohort included five tumor types: 81 colorectal organoids from 79 donors, 49 esophageal organoids from 49 donors, 11 ovarian organoids from 9 donors, 4 pancreatic organoids from 4 donors, and 3 gastric organoids from 3 donors. RNA-seq input and experimental CRISPR coverage for all 20 locked targets were available for every included organoid. Colorectal and esophageal subgroup analyses were prespecified as secondary, the ovarian subgroup was exploratory, and the pancreatic and gastric subgroups were treated as descriptive because of their small sample sizes. The primary external-validation analysis used all 148 organoids, with donor structure preserved in uncertainty estimation and sensitivity analyses. Sanger TPM values were transformed as log2(TPM + 1) and aligned to the frozen expression-only feature space using Entrez Gene identifiers. For each target, the 300 expression features stored in the corresponding 2D deployment-model bundle were used in their locked order. Across targets, 3790 of 3845 unique locked genes were measured in the Sanger matrix; 55 genes were absent. Target-specific coverage ranged from 291 to 300 of 300 features (97.0–100%), exceeding the prespecified 80% minimum for all targets. Missing features were neither re-estimated from the external cohort nor replaced using Sanger-derived values; only the imputation and standardization objects learned in 2D data and the fixed Elastic Net coefficients were applied. A pre-outcome audit identified that the original coverage-key array had incorrectly counted the 55 entirely absent genes as present. The coverage metadata were corrected before outcome access, while the original and corrected blinded predictions remained bitwise identical. Before the Sanger CRISPR outcomes were opened, the 148-organoid × 20-target blinded prediction matrix, cohort registry, protocol, model manifest, and feature manifest were written and sealed with SHA-256 digests; all 11 prespecified seal checks passed. Only after this lock was established were the Sanger outcomes accessed once and merged with the predictions in the frozen sample and target order, yielding 2960 finite prediction-outcome pairs. The external outcome was the corrected and scaled gene-level CRISPR LFC, with more negative values indicating stronger dependency. For the 13 organoids screened with two CRISPR libraries, the official Sanger covariate was applied: minLib was selected when the code was 1 and v1_1 when the code was 0. The available quality-controlled screen was used for single-library organoids. This externally defined selection rule was fixed before inspection of the target outcomes. The primary external-validation estimand was the target-specific Spearman correlation between the blinded predictions and Sanger LFC values, followed by the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). This primary estimand evaluates the ordering of organoids for a fixed gene and does not assess the ordering of genes within an organoid. Rank correlation was selected because the Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values are on assay-specific scales and were not assumed to be numerically comparable. Uncertainty was estimated with 5000 donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, retaining all organoids from a sampled donor together; 95% CIs were obtained from the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. For the null distribution, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, predictions were held fixed, and each donor-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was permuted within tumor-type/lineage strata for 5000 iterations. The two-sided empirical  p -value was calculated as (1 + the number of absolute null macro correlations at least as large as the observed absolute macro correlation)/5001. Target-specific permutation  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. A lineage-only comparator, frozen before access to Sanger outcomes and based on patient-weighted 2D lineage means, was applied to the same cohort. We evaluated the expression-only-minus-lineage macro difference using paired donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, and we also calculated partial Spearman correlations adjusted for the primary tumor-type indicators. The influence of repeated donors was examined in two additional analyses: one retained the lexicographically first sample identifier per donor, and the other averaged predictions and outcomes within donor. An independent replay recomputed cohort size, donor count, overlap exclusions, feature coverage, target and prediction order, the complete 2960-pair grid, correlation statistics, bootstrap and permutation results, lineage adjustment, and file hashes; all 22 checks passed. As detailed in  Table S6 , no model, target, feature, hyperparameter, or prespecified primary metric was changed after outcome unlock. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints were evaluated separately using the unchanged predictions.

SECTION 2.8. Retrospective Cross-Cohort Transportability Synthesis The target-specific results from the Broad-94 exploratory evaluation and the Sanger-148 blinded independent external validation were combined retrospectively only after the two cohort-specific analyses had been completed [ 38 , 39 ]. The fixed 20-target panel defined in  Section 2.2  was aligned using Entrez Gene identifiers, and no targets were removed based on effect direction, magnitude, or statistical significance. Because Broad used Chronos Gene Effect values and Sanger used corrected CRISPR LFC values, the synthesis addressed target-specific rank transportability rather than absolute-scale equivalence. The Tiriac pancreatic projection was excluded from this synthesis because it did not contain measured genome-scale CRISPR outcomes. The synthesis protocol is summarized in  Table S7 . Directional concordance was defined as a positive Spearman correlation for the same target in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148. The number of common-positive targets was compared with a one-sided exact binomial distribution with  n  = 20 and a null success probability of 0.50. This comparison was interpreted only as a descriptive sign benchmark: dependence among targets limits the binomial interpretation, and the synthesis was defined after the cohort-specific results were known. Agreement in relative target performance was evaluated separately using the two-sided Spearman correlation between the vectors of 20 Broad and 20 Sanger target-specific correlations. For target-level pooling, each cohort-specific Spearman correlation was transformed to the Fisher-z scale. We derived inverse-variance weights from the cohort-specific uncertainty intervals obtained from the lineage-stratified Broad bootstrap and the donor-clustered Sanger bootstrap. Because Broad donor structure was incompletely ascertained, its original uncertainty weights may overstate precision; the synthesis is conditional on this unresolved model-level independence assumption. A fixed-effects pooled estimate was calculated for each target and then transformed back to the Spearman correlation scale. The resulting two-sided meta-analytic  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. Because only two 3D cohorts were available, random-effects estimates and the I 2  heterogeneity statistic were retained as descriptive sensitivity measures and were not used as confirmatory decision criteria. The panel-wide combined 3D estimate was defined as the arithmetic mean of the 20 fixed-effect target correlations. Sensitivity to target-panel composition was assessed by resampling the 20 locked targets with replacement for 20,000 iterations, then recalculating the combined macro correlation at each iteration. We reported the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles as a descriptive 95% interval; this interval characterizes sensitivity to the composition of the fixed target panel and is not a new-patient or new-organoid sampling interval. Attenuation from 2D to 3D was calculated for each target by subtracting the corresponding locked 2D holdout correlation from the Broad or Sanger correlation, with panel-level summaries obtained using the same target-bootstrap structure. To evaluate whether cross-target dependence could explain the common-positive count, we performed a post hoc, correlation-aware simultaneous permutation analysis. Predictions remained fixed. In Broad, each ModelID-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was retained as a block; in Sanger, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, and each donor-level 20-target outcome vector was retained as a block. These vectors were permuted simultaneously among units within each cohort for 50,000 iterations, preserving the cross-target covariance structure. We recalculated the number of targets remaining positive in both cohorts at each iteration and calculated the one-sided empirical  p -value with the plus-one correction. This analysis was explicitly treated as a post hoc sensitivity analysis and did not constitute a third external validation. Within each cohort, we subtracted the target-specific lineage-only correlation from the corresponding expression-only correlation. We used a one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the 20 paired differences to assess the incremental contribution of expression beyond lineage. A retrospective evidence classification was used for reporting only. Tier A required a positive correlation in both cohorts, a meta-analytic FDR q < 0.05, and target-level FDR q < 0.05 in at least one individual cohort. Tier B indicated positive correlations in both cohorts without fulfillment of all Tier A criteria. Tier C indicated a non-positive correlation in at least one cohort. As specified in  Table S7 , the evidence tiers did not alter the locked panel or any model component, and no retraining, target or feature reselection, hyperparameter tuning, metric switching, or direction-based target removal was permitted during the synthesis.

SECTION 2.9. Secondary Transcriptomic Projection in the Tiriac Pancreatic Organoid Cohort After completion of 2D model development, Broad-94 exploration, Sanger-148 blinded validation, and the retrospective cross-cohort synthesis, the frozen expression-only deployment models were applied to the Tiriac/ORGANOID-PANCREATIC cohort as a secondary biological projection [ 32 ]. Because measured genome-scale CRISPR dependency outcomes were unavailable, this analysis was prespecified as an application to an independent pancreatic transcriptome rather than external validation; predictive accuracy and CRISPR performance were not estimated. Fifty-five publicly available RNA-seq Spliced Transcripts Alignment to a Reference (STAR) count files from the GDC project were obtained from the official public Amazon Simple Storage Service mirror. The cohort comprised 44 tumor samples representing 38 independent tumor cases and 11 samples from 11 independent normal cases; group membership was defined from the source TissueType metadata. Cohort preparation and projection procedures are summarized in  Table S8 . The tpm_unstranded field in the STAR output was used as the abundance measure. Rows mapping to the same gene symbol were summed at the TPM level before log2(TPM + 1) transformation. The resulting sample-by-feature matrix was aligned to the locked expression features of the 20 target-specific Elastic Net deployment bundles finalized in all 852 2D models. The target panel, target-specific feature identities, stored imputation and standardization objects, hyperparameters, coefficients, and projection definitions were applied without modification. No Tiriac sample contributed to model fitting, feature selection, recalibration, retuning, or endpoint definition. Of the 3845 unique locked features used across the 20 models, 3806 were observed in the Tiriac data. Target-specific coverage ranged from 292 to 300 of 300 selected features (97.33–100%), and every target met the prespecified 80% coverage threshold. Features absent from the Tiriac matrix were represented as not-a-number (NaN) values, filled with the medians stored in the frozen all-852 deployment models before the stored standardization, and then coefficients were applied. This preserved the training preprocessing rule without estimating replacement values from Tiriac. An independent computational replay reproduced the archived predictions exactly using training-median imputation ( Section 2.10.2 ). This procedure produced a 55-sample × 20-target projection matrix. Source file identifiers, file sizes, source-provided integrity values, local SHA-256 digests, model and feature manifests, and prediction records were retained for provenance. When multiple tumor organoid samples originated from the same GDC case, the 20 sample-level projections were averaged within the case, and the patient/case was used as the inferential unit. For each target, median case-level projections were calculated for 38 tumor cases and 11 normal cases, together with the tumor-minus-normal median difference. Uncertainty in this difference was estimated using 5000 case-level bootstrap resamples within the tumor and normal groups, with 95% percentile intervals. Tumor and normal projection distributions were compared using two-sided Mann–Whitney U tests, and the 20  p -values were adjusted using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. Cliff’s delta was reported as an effect-size measure; under the prespecified orientation, a positive value indicated a more negative, and therefore stronger, projected dependency distribution in tumors. These analyses compared frozen model outputs rather than measured dependencies or model errors. The 20-target median projection vector for Tiriac tumor cases was compared separately with the corresponding median prediction vectors for Broad-94 and Sanger-148. This comparison assessed whether the projected pancreatic target hierarchy was consistent across organoid applications. Concordance was quantified using two-sided Spearman correlations across the 20 targets. Because all compared values were predictions from the same locked model family and Tiriac lacked measured CRISPR outcomes, these correlations were interpreted as secondary evidence of target-ranking consistency rather than independent evidence of predictive accuracy. Expression-domain shift was evaluated across the 3790 locked expression features that are jointly available in Tiriac, Broad-94, and Sanger-148. Multivariable separation among cohorts was visualized by principal component analysis (PCA). For Tiriac versus Broad-94 and Tiriac versus Sanger-148, a standardized mean difference (SMD) was calculated for each common feature, and shift magnitude was summarized using the median absolute SMD and the proportions of features with |SMD| > 1 and |SMD| > 2. The PCA and SMD diagnostics were descriptive and were not used to recalibrate the models, delete features, remove targets, or revise projections.  Table S8  defines the corresponding claim boundary: Tiriac results were described as projections or prioritizations and not as validated dependencies, confirmed dependencies, or therapeutic sensitivity.

SECTION 3.1. Selection of the Expression-Only Model in the 2D Development Cohort Five input representations—the lineage-only reference and four molecular candidates—were compared in the development partition of 681 models representing 658 patient groups, using identical patient-grouped outer folds. Candidate-level OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlations and paired differences relative to expression-only are shown in  Figure 2 a,b; full candidate- and target-level results are provided in  Table S10 . Figure 2 Development-cohort omics ablation and selection of the expression-only model. ( a ) Patient-grouped OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlations for the lineage-only reference and four molecular candidate representations in the 681-model development cohort. Error bars show 95% percentile confidence intervals from 2000 PatientID-cluster bootstrap replicates. The blue square denotes the expression-only candidate selected by the prespecified one-standard-error rule. ( b ) Paired differences in macro Spearman correlation between each augmented molecular representation and expression-only. Error bars show 95% paired PatientID-cluster bootstrap intervals; the vertical dashed line denotes no difference. CI, confidence interval; OOF, out-of-fold. As shown in  Figure 2 a, the highest observed OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlation was obtained with expression + copy number (ρ = 0.4081; 95% CI, 0.3894–0.4243). Performance was nearly identical for expression-only (ρ = 0.4071; 95% CI, 0.3893–0.4232), expression + mutation (ρ = 0.4073; 95% CI, 0.3908–0.4235), and all omics + lineage (ρ = 0.4031; 95% CI, 0.3864–0.4184). The lineage-only reference produced a lower macro correlation (ρ = 0.2598; 95% CI, 0.2388–0.2806) and, as prespecified, was not eligible for molecular candidate selection. Relative to expression-only, paired PatientID-cluster bootstrap differences were +0.0010 (95% CI, −0.0047 to 0.0066) for expression + copy number, +0.0002 (95% CI, −0.0075 to 0.0079) for expression + mutation, and −0.0039 (95% CI, −0.0123 to 0.0043) for all omics + lineage ( Figure 2 b). All three intervals included zero, providing no evidence that adding copy-number, mutation, or combined omic and lineage information produced a reliable improvement in development performance. The expression-only candidate remained within one bootstrap standard error of the highest-scoring molecular candidate and showed the greatest mean feature-set Jaccard stability across outer folds (0.565 versus 0.550–0.554 for the other molecular candidates). The prespecified one-standard-error rule therefore selected expression-only as the final molecular representation.

SECTION 3.5. Retrospective Cross-Cohort Transportability Synthesis After the Broad-94 exploratory analysis and the Sanger-148 blinded external validation had been completed separately, their target-specific results were retrospectively combined for the unchanged 20-gene panel. The synthesis evaluated the direction and magnitude of rank transfer rather than the absolute agreement between the Broad Chronos Gene Effect and Sanger-corrected CRISPR LFC scales. Panel-wide and target-level synthesis results are summarized in  Figure 6  and  Table S14 . Figure 6 Retrospective cross-cohort transportability synthesis across Broad-94 and Sanger-148 organoids. ( a ) Macro correlations for the locked 2D holdout, Broad-94, Sanger-148, and the fixed-effect combined 3D estimate. Error bars show cohort-specific 95% bootstrap confidence intervals and, for the combined 3D estimate, a descriptive 95% target-panel bootstrap interval. ( b ) Target-wise Broad-94 versus Sanger-148 correlations, classified as Tier A, B, or C. ( c ) Fixed-effect target-level meta-analytic correlations with 95% confidence intervals. ( d ) Evidence-tier counts and sensitivity summaries. The exact-binomial benchmark was descriptive, and the 50,000-iteration correlation-aware permutation was post hoc; neither constituted a new external validation. In ( a ), gray, green, purple, and brown points and error bars denote the locked 2D holdout, Broad-94, Sanger-148, and combined 3D estimates, respectively. In ( b , c ), blue circles, orange squares, and gray triangles denote Tiers A, B, and C, respectively; the same tier colors are used for the bars in ( d ). Solid black reference lines in ( a – c ) mark zero correlation. 2D, two-dimensional; 3D, three-dimensional; BH, Benjamini–Hochberg; CI, confidence interval. As shown in  Figure 6 a, the arithmetic mean of the 20 fixed-effect target-level meta-analytic correlations was 0.1231, with a 20,000-replicate target-panel bootstrap interval of 0.0769–0.1704. This combined 3D estimate was substantially lower than the locked 2D holdout correlation of 0.4094; the combined 3D-minus-locked-2D difference was −0.2863. The results therefore indicated measurable transfer of dependency ranking into organoids, accompanied by marked attenuation relative to performance within the 2D domain. This observed cross-domain performance contrast does not identify the biological or technical causes of attenuation. Fifteen of the 20 frozen targets had positive correlations in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148 (75%). A one-sided exact binomial comparison against a null success probability of 0.50 yielded  p  = 0.0207. The targets were correlated, and we defined the synthesis after the cohort-specific results were known. We therefore interpreted this value only as a descriptive benchmark. In the post hoc correlation-aware sensitivity analysis, Sanger outcomes were first averaged within donors, and each unit’s complete 20-target outcome vector was permuted as a block. Fourteen of 20 targets remained positive in both cohorts, and 50,000 simultaneous permutations yielded a one-sided empirical  p  = 1.0 × 10 −4  ( Figure 6 d). As shown in  Figure 6 b,c and  Table S14 , eight targets met the combined meta-analytic Benjamini–Hochberg threshold of q < 0.05. The reporting classification comprised three Tier A, 12 Tier B, and five Tier C targets. The strongest Tier A support was observed for  CCNE1  (Broad ρ = 0.3456; Sanger ρ = 0.2792; combined ρ = 0.3128; 95% CI, 0.1865–0.4290; q = 5.18 × 10 −5 ),  CDK6  (0.2447; 0.3221; combined ρ = 0.2924; 95% CI, 0.1660–0.4094; q = 1.01 × 10 −4 ), and  MDM4  (0.2000; 0.2992; combined ρ = 0.2655; 95% CI, 0.1400–0.3825; q = 2.36 × 10 −4 ). These targets combined positive transfer in both cohorts, multiplicity-adjusted meta-analytic support, and target-level FDR support in at least one individual cohort. The correlation between the vectors of target-specific Broad and Sanger performance estimates was ρ = 0.2782 (two-sided  p  = 0.2350), indicating that panel-wide directional replication did not imply stable relative performance ordering across cohorts ( Figure 6 b). The expression-only-minus-lineage differences were positive at the panel level in Broad-94 (one-sided Wilcoxon  p  = 0.0379) but did not reach the corresponding threshold in Sanger-148 ( p  = 0.1012). Thus, the retrospective synthesis strengthened evidence for limited cross-cohort directionality but did not constitute a third external validation or support equivalent target performance across the two organoid resources.

SECTION 4. Discussion This study tested whether transcriptome-based gene-dependency rankings learned in 2D cancer models retained a detectable but attenuated signal in patient-derived 3D organoids. Transfer was evaluated without outcome-guided retraining. The findings provide limited support for this hypothesis. We selected the expression-only representation in the development cohort. Holdout performance provided internal confirmation conditional on historical target selection. Positive panel-wide rank signals were observed in the Broad-94 exploratory cohort and the Sanger-148 blinded independent external validation cohort. However, macro correlations in Broad, Sanger, and the combined 3D analysis were markedly lower than in the locked 2D holdout. Transfer was therefore incomplete and weak. The findings support modest, context-sensitive preservation of organoid ranking for genes within the preselected panel across independently generated resources. They do not establish cross-platform equivalence. This primary finding is distinct from the secondary within-organoid gene-ranking results in  Section 3.7 . Tumor lineage is central to interpreting this transfer. Expression patterns can encode tissue identity and shared transcriptional programs. A model may therefore reproduce differences in dependency between tumor types without identifying the mechanisms responsible for those dependencies. The CRISPR outcomes measure gene-depletion effects, but the fitted expression–dependency relationships remain predictive associations. Their transfer does not establish that the predictive expression features have a causal role in determining dependency. Thus, predictive generalization to organoids and mechanistic generalization are distinct claims. For the prespecified target-wise endpoint, the added predictive value of transcriptomic modeling over tumor lineage was not established in the blinded Sanger cohort. The expression-only model exceeded the frozen lineage-only comparator numerically by 0.0462 in macro Spearman correlation. However, the paired donor-cluster bootstrap CI included zero (95% CI, −0.0065 to 0.0986). This result establishes neither superiority nor equivalence. The significant within-lineage permutation result addresses a different question: whether the prediction–outcome association exceeds a null distribution that preserves tumor-type structure. It does not establish superior performance over the lineage-only predictor. Target-specific correlations rank organoids for a given gene. They cannot by themselves establish correct gene ranking within an organoid or an increase in the yield of functional experiments. The study contributes an outcome-blinded assessment of performance attenuation from cell lines to organoids and documents uncertainty in the incremental value of transcriptomic prediction.  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1  remain candidates for prospective investigation. Their multiplicity-adjusted correlations do not establish target-specific superiority over lineage or practical benefit. The retrospective within-organoid analysis directly examined this second ranking task. Full 20-gene rankings improved over a fixed 2D-mean ranking and, more modestly, over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts ( Table S17 ). These comparisons are needed because shared differences in average dependency among genes can produce positive within-organoid correlations. A gain in full-panel correlation did not ensure a better short candidate list. Top-three overlap exceeded lineage-only prediction in Sanger but not in Broad ( Table S18 ). The donor-mean sensitivity analysis gave similar Sanger results ( Table S19 ). This is limited retrospective evidence of ranking agreement within a selected panel, not prospective proof that transcriptomic modeling improves experimental yield. It neither replaces the original primary analysis nor resolves its uncertainty about target-wise superiority over lineage. Adding copy-number, mutation, and lineage information to gene expression did not reliably improve development performance. DeepDEP and transcriptome-based maps developed for The Cancer Genome Atlas use molecular profiles from cancer cell lines to estimate dependencies in functionally unscreened tumors [ 16 , 17 ]. Synthetic data augmentation and multi-omic integration have also been proposed to address incomplete profiles and capture more complex dependency patterns [ 18 ]. The expression-plus-copy-number candidate yielded the highest development score numerically. However, its advantage over expression-only was negligible, and the paired bootstrap interval included zero. The expression-only representation also showed the greatest feature-set stability and was selected by the prespecified one-standard-error rule. This result does not imply that copy number or mutation is biologically uninformative. This finding applies to the fixed 20-target panel, the available sample size, the 300-feature limit per target, and the linear Elastic Net framework. Within these constraints, the additional data layers did not provide a sufficiently stable incremental signal to justify greater complexity. The broad availability and relatively consistent measurement of transcriptomic data across model collections further strengthens the practical rationale for an expression-only deployment model. Performance was lower in the organoid cohorts than in the 2D internal confirmation analysis. The macro Spearman correlation was 0.4094 in the locked 2D holdout, compared with 0.1518 in Broad-94 and 0.1015 in Sanger-148. This attenuation is interpreted as an observed cross-domain effect whose biological and technical causes remain uncertain. Computational alignment studies have documented transcriptional differences between cell lines and tumors [ 20 ]. Next-generation model resources have reported tumor-relevant transcriptional programs and culture-specific dependencies in organoids and spheroids [ 38 ], while patient-derived model collections have shown that culture conditions can modify cellular states in a subset of models [ 40 ]. These studies provide context for plausible explanations, but they do not establish the causes of the performance decline in the present cohorts. Differences in cellular state, growth geometry, medium composition, lineage composition, and functional-screening platforms may contribute individually or jointly. Their contributions were not experimentally disentangled or quantified here. The comparison used different cohorts and assay settings, rather than paired models in which growth conditions and screening platforms were varied under controlled conditions. It therefore cannot attribute the decline to 3D growth itself, identify a dominant mechanism, or exclude contributions from model limitations and cohort composition. Historical target-panel selection also limits interpretation of the 2D reference estimate. The observed attenuation supports evaluating dependency models across data domains without assigning a confirmed biological or technical explanation to the decline. The contrasting roles of Broad-94 and Sanger-148 were central to the evidential interpretation. Broad-94 was retained as exploratory because the resource had been accessible during earlier methodological work and donor information was absent from the original evaluation records. The later audit recovered only partial donor information and confirmed a same-donor pair ( Tables S22–S24 ); it did not establish independence of the full cohort. The audited analysis run nevertheless preserved a prediction-before-outcome sequence. Sanger-148, by contrast, was constructed after excluding Broad-linked and same-donor records, and its predictions were cryptographically sealed before the CRISPR outcomes were opened. Donor-clustered uncertainty estimation and donor-aware permutation further preserved the strongest available biological independence structure. Differences in screening libraries, protocols, and computational processing can affect Broad and Sanger dependency estimates [ 10 , 11 ]. Chronos was developed in part to model sources of variation in CRISPR screen dynamics [ 12 ]. Target-wise correlations were positive for 18 of 20 targets in Broad-94 and 17 of 20 in Sanger-148. This pattern suggests that the panel-wide direction was not confined to a single laboratory or processing system. Nevertheless, the Sanger target-wise expression-only-minus-lineage CI included zero, so that cohort did not establish an incremental advantage on the prespecified primary endpoint. Transcriptomic information therefore carried a detectable external rank signal, but part of that signal may reflect lineage-associated structure. The target-level findings provided the strongest cross-cohort support for  CCNE1 ,  CDK6 , and  MDM4 .  CCNE1  was positive in both organoid cohorts, passed the FDR threshold in Sanger-148, and yielded one of the largest combined estimates. This is biologically consistent with the recent Sanger organoid dependency map, in which  CCNE1  amplification in esophageal organoids was associated with  CCNE1  dependency [ 39 ].  CDK6  produced the highest target-specific correlation in Sanger-148 and remained in Tier A in the retrospective synthesis. The Broad NextGen study reported enrichment for  CDK6  dependency in glioblastoma models that preserve a tumor-proximal glial transcriptional state. Genetic and pharmacological experiments supported this association in specific genomic contexts [ 38 ]. That work provides an example of how transcriptional state can expose context-dependent  CDK6  vulnerability.  MDM4  also showed a positive ranking signal in both cohorts. However, the current reference set provides less direct organoid-based mechanistic support for  MDM4  than for  CCNE1  or  CDK6 .  MDM4  should therefore be regarded primarily as a hypothesis for targeted functional testing. These findings identify genes whose variation across organoids was predicted with the strongest support. They do not establish that these are the three strongest dependencies within an individual organoid. More generally, their target-specific correlations do not demonstrate drug sensitivity, therapeutic index, or clinical benefit. The retrospective synthesis distinguished directional replication at the panel level from stability in the relative ordering of target performance. Fifteen of the 20 targets were positive in both cohorts. The directionally concordant count remained above the correlation-aware null expectation when complete target vectors were permuted as blocks. In contrast, the correlation between the Broad and Sanger vectors of target-specific performance was only 0.2782 and was not statistically significant. Most targets therefore transferred in the same direction. This does not imply that they were predicted with equivalent strength in both organoid resources. The Tier A–C framework summarized these differences. Tier A identified the three targets with replicated direction, combined multiplicity-adjusted support, and individual-cohort evidence. Tier B captured directionally concordant but less strongly supported signals. Tier C indicated non-positive transfer in at least one cohort. Because these tiers were assigned retrospectively after the cohort-specific results were known, they should be treated as reporting categories rather than validated biological classes. Their reproducibility must be tested prospectively in a new cohort using prespecified criteria. The Tiriac pancreatic organoid analysis provided a complementary application layer rather than an additional validation experiment. Pancreatic organoids have been shown to preserve important disease features and treatment-response patterns [ 30 , 31 , 32 , 33 ]. The Tiriac tumor-case target ranking was concordant with the median prediction rankings in Broad and Sanger. Thus, the frozen model family produced a broadly consistent target hierarchy in an independent pancreatic transcriptomic resource. However, all compared values were predictions from the same model family, and Tiriac provided no measured genome-scale CRISPR outcomes. The observed concordance therefore reflects cross-cohort consistency of model outputs, not predictive accuracy. Moreover, principal component separation and large SMDs across the shared expression features indicated substantial domain shift.  CTNNB1  had the most negative pancreatic projection but was not a Tier A target in the CRISPR synthesis.  MDM4  was projected to be more negative in normal than tumor cases. These findings further illustrate why the outputs should not be interpreted as a context-independent hierarchy of biological dependency. The retrospective missing-feature sensitivity analysis showed high overall agreement with the original median-imputed Tiriac projections. However, zero filling changed the top-three set in two of 38 tumor cases, and individual target shifts reached 0.1077 Gene Effect units. Training-reference mean filling preserved all tumor-case top-three sets, but this agreement cannot establish greater accuracy. Constant per-target offsets explain the unchanged tumor–normal contrasts. These findings support reporting the preprocessing rule explicitly and retaining the projections as hypotheses for functional testing ( Tables S20 and S21 ). The computational framework may help select direct organoid experiments, but its utility requires prospective testing. Genome-scale or large-scale CRISPR studies in intestinal, colon, and gastric organoids have identified context-specific genes related to growth, treatment resistance, and gene-drug interactions [ 34 , 35 , 36 , 37 ]. The larger Sanger organoid biobank has likewise mapped dependencies linked to genomic and clinical features through direct perturbation [ 39 ]. Such experiments provide stronger evidence of functional causality than computational projections, but remain technically demanding and resource-intensive. Frozen expression models could be evaluated as a low-cost pre-screen for allocating scarce CRISPR or pharmacological validation capacity. The within-organoid analysis tests the relative ordering of measured scores within 20 genes. It does not establish a common calibrated dependency scale across independently fitted gene models. Its modest full-ranking gains and cohort-dependent top-three results require prospective confirmation before claiming improved target prioritization. Several design features strengthen the study. Patient-grouped nested cross-validation reduced leakage between biologically related samples. The one-standard-error rule limited unnecessary complexity when candidate performance was nearly equivalent [ 22 , 23 ]. The locked 2D evaluation, the subsequent all-852 deployment refit, the Broad exploratory application, and the Sanger blinded external validation were kept analytically distinct. Targets, feature identities, hyperparameters, and the primary metric were not reopened in response to 3D outcomes. Prediction-before-outcome locks, SHA-256 manifests, independent computational replay, and bootstrap and permutation schemes matched to the available biological unit reduced opportunities for retrospective optimization. Retaining all 20 targets irrespective of effect direction or statistical significance also reduced selective reporting. The accompanying reproducibility package allows readers to trace the reported results to frozen predictions, analysis records, and integrity checks. The study also has important limitations. First, historical selection of the fixed 20-gene panel used all 852 2D models. The 171-model holdout was independent of the current candidate representation, feature selection, hyperparameters, and model-fitting decisions. However, it was not fully independent of historical target selection. The holdout therefore provides internal confirmation conditional on that selection, not an unbiased estimate of generalization for the complete analysis pipeline. Selection-induced optimism cannot be excluded or quantified by the reported fixed-panel analysis. Second, all external performance estimates are restricted to the preselected 20 targets. Sample independence in Sanger does not establish dependency transferability for unselected genes or across the genome. Target-panel bootstrap intervals quantify sensitivity to panel composition rather than sampling uncertainty for new patients or organoids. Third, donor metadata could be recovered for only 25 of the 94 Broad models, and one same-donor pair was confirmed. The remaining 69 models and cross-source donor equivalence could not be resolved. The partial-cluster and donor-documented-subset sensitivities do not establish full-cohort independence. Original Broad CIs, permutation results and the uncertainty weights used in retrospective synthesis remain conditional on model-level independence and may overstate precision if additional dependence is present. Consequently, Broad and Sanger uncertainty estimates and effective sample sizes are not directly comparable. Fourth, Sanger-148 was dominated by colorectal and esophageal organoids. Ovarian, pancreatic, and gastric subgroups were too small to establish lineage-specific generalizability. Fifth, the differing Broad Chronos Gene Effect and Sanger-corrected LFC scales precluded direct absolute-error and calibration comparisons [ 10 , 11 , 12 ]. Sixth, target-specific linear expression-only models cannot fully represent genotype-transcriptome interactions, network structure, cell–cell interactions, or the incompletely modeled stromal and immune compartments of organoid systems [ 24 , 25 , 29 ]. Finally, the Tiriac normal group contained only 11 cases and lacked measured CRISPR dependencies, matched drug-response outcomes, and clinical endpoints. The study consequently establishes rank-transfer evidence, not prospective therapeutic utility. Adjustment for broad tumor-type labels and comparison with a lineage-only reference cannot fully separate lineage-associated transcriptional structure from other predictive signals. The present analyses therefore do not establish the causal basis of the transferred associations. Lineage adjustment and descriptive expression-domain diagnostics do not experimentally separate the causes of performance attenuation. The present design cannot estimate the individual contributions of culture conditions, cellular state, cohort composition, and screening technology to the observed cross-domain effect. The within-organoid endpoints introduce additional limitations. They were specified retrospectively after outcomes were available, and their CIs were not adjusted for multiple secondary comparisons. The fixed panel and frozen-model analysis do not establish genome-wide ranking performance or quantify uncertainty from retraining or selecting a different gene panel. Additional same-donor dependence remains unresolved in Broad despite the partial-donor secondary ranking sensitivities in  Table S24 . Finally, agreement with CRISPR score ordering is not evidence of cancer selectivity, druggability, therapeutic benefit, or increased success of prospectively chosen experiments. Future work should test the Tier A targets using direct CRISPR perturbation in preregistered, tumor-type-balanced organoid cohorts. Gene-depletion effects should be related to target-specific inhibitor responses and, where available, to matched clinical information. Distinguishing biological from technical contributions to attenuation would require larger cohorts with patient-level identifiers, paired 2D and 3D models, controlled variation in culture conditions, and harmonized or cross-platform CRISPR measurements. Nonlinear models incorporating network or pathway structure, domain-adaptation strategies, and richer multi-omic representations are also reasonable candidates for evaluation. Their value, however, should be assessed without outcome-guided retuning and under the same prediction-lock and independent-validation discipline used here. The results document modest rank transfer within the selected gene panel. Whether the retrospective within-organoid ranking gains improve prospective experimental prioritization beyond tumor lineage remains to be established.

SECTION Data Availability Statement This study analyzed publicly available or openly released datasets. The 2D molecular profiles and CRISPR Gene Effect data were obtained from the DepMap Public 26Q1 release and the CCLE [ 3 , 4 , 13 , 14 ] ( https://depmap.org/portal/data_page/?tab=allData ) (accessed on 26 September 2026). The Broad NextGen organoid data were obtained from the  NextGen Model Manuscript 2026  resource and the associated Figshare dataset [ 38 ] ( https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.29472362.v1 ). The Sanger tumor-derived organoid dependency data were obtained from the associated Figshare dataset [ 39 ] ( https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.28339340 ). The Tiriac pancreatic organoid RNA-seq data were obtained from the GDC ORGANOID-PANCREATIC project [ 32 ] ( https://portal.gdc.cancer.gov/projects/ORGANOID-PANCREATIC ) (accessed on 26 September 2026). The analysis code, trained models, processed data, and supporting results are available in the study’s reproducibility repository on Zenodo [ 41 ]. Original source datasets remain available from their respective repositories under the applicable access and licensing conditions.
