Identifying genes cancer cells need to survive or grow may help researchers choose experimental targets. Direct testing is difficult and costly in organoids, which are small three-dimensional models grown from patient tumors. Using gene activity measurements from 852 conventional cancer models, we built predictive models for a preselected panel of 20 genes. Our primary evaluation asked whether the models could rank organoids by their dependence on each gene. Performance was weaker in organoids, but a small signal remained in 94 Broad organoids and in 148 Sanger organoids from 144 donors. Predictions for the Sanger organoids were fixed before the measured outcomes were accessed. A separate retrospective analysis examined whether the same predictions could rank the 20 genes within each organoid. This retrospective analysis showed modest improvement over tumor-lineage predictions for the full ranking, while improvement for the three strongest measured dependencies was observed only in Sanger. Pancreatic organoid results remained projections because direct measurements were unavailable. These findings provide limited evidence for ranking genes within the selected panel. They do not establish which experiments will succeed or which treatments will work in patients. 

Transferability of transcriptome-based gene-dependency models from two-dimensional cancer models to organoids remains uncertain. Expression-only Elastic Net models for 20 preselected genes were selected using patient-grouped nested cross-validation in 681 models and evaluated in a locked 171-model holdout. Historical target selection used all 852 models, making holdout performance conditional internal confirmation rather than an unbiased generalization estimate. After refitting on all 852 models, frozen models underwent exploratory evaluation in 94 Broad organoids and blinded validation in 148 Sanger organoids from 144 donors. Predictions were sealed before Sanger outcome access. Primary target-wise Spearman correlations, ranking organoids per gene, averaged 0.4094, 0.1518, and 0.1015 in holdout, Broad, and Sanger, respectively. Sanger association exceeded the within-lineage permutation null ( p  = 0.00040), but improvement over lineage-only prediction remained uncertain (difference, 0.0462; 95% confidence interval, −0.0065–0.0986).  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1  passed false discovery rate correction in Sanger. Retrospective within-organoid gene ranking modestly improved over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts; top-three overlap improved only in Sanger. Pancreatic results remained unvalidated projections. Findings support weak cross-domain rank transfer and limited within-organoid ranking within the selected panel, without establishing genome-wide transferability, causal explanations for attenuation, or prospective experimental or therapeutic utility. 

Cancer comprises a heterogeneous group of diseases driven not only by genetic and epigenetic alterations but also by the reorganization of cellular signaling networks. This heterogeneity contributes to variable treatment responses among tumors with the same histological diagnosis and limits the effectiveness of target selection based solely on genomic alterations [ 1 ]. Gene dependency refers to a cancer cell’s requirement to express a particular gene to survive or proliferate. Identifying such dependencies and synthetic-lethal relationships offers a strategy for exposing cancer-specific vulnerabilities while comparatively sparing normal cells [ 2 ]. 

The Cancer Cell Line Encyclopedia (CCLE) has established a molecular foundation for modeling associations among mutation, copy number, gene expression, treatment response, and gene dependency [ 13 , 14 ]. Although proteomic profiling expands this information space, messenger RNA expression remains an accessible readout of cellular state, measurable consistently across many cohorts [ 15 ]. This availability has motivated the computational prediction of dependencies in samples without direct functional screening. DeepDEP demonstrated that multi-omic profiles can predict cancer dependencies [ 16 ], while transcriptome-based dependency maps developed for The Cancer Genome Atlas showed that Elastic Net models can prioritize putative tumor vulnerabilities [ 17 ]. Other approaches have used synthetic data augmentation to address incomplete molecular profiles [ 18 ]. However, expression-dependency associations can reflect tissue of origin and shared transcriptional programs [ 19 ], and domain shifts between cell lines and tumors can compromise transferability [ 20 ]. Thus, strong performance within cell-line collections should not be equated with generalizability across biological model systems. Elastic Net is well suited to high-dimensional data because it combines regularization, variable selection, and stability in the presence of correlated features [ 21 ]. Yet model selection and performance estimation on the same observations can produce optimistic bias, necessitating nested cross-validation [ 22 ]. Likewise, samples derived from the same patient or biological source must remain within a common data partition to prevent information leakage [ 23 ]. 

Direct functional screening in organoids is technically challenging, yet genome-scale studies in intestinal organoids have identified genes associated with growth and treatment resistance [ 34 ]. Pooled screens in human colon organoids have revealed tumor-suppressor and context-dependent selection effects [ 35 ], and more recent work has identified dependencies involving WNT, mitogen-activated protein kinase, phosphoinositide 3-kinase, p53, and deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) mismatch-repair pathways [ 36 ]. Large-scale CRISPR knockout, CRISPR interference, and CRISPR activation screens in gastric organoids have further demonstrated the feasibility of examining gene-drug interactions in 3D systems [ 37 ]. Next-generation model collections show that dependency profiles may differ between 2D and 3D culture conditions [ 38 ]. Cancer dependencies can also be mapped systematically across tumor organoid biobanks [ 39 ]. Expanded patient-derived model resources can capture substantial tumor diversity [ 40 ]. Together, these studies establish organoids as increasingly tractable functional genomics platforms while also underscoring that culture context can alter the biological relationships inferred from conventional cell lines. 

Despite these advances, the extent to which transcriptome-dependency relationships learned in 2D cancer cell lines transfer to independent 3D organoid cohorts remains unresolved. A rigorous assessment requires model selection to be completed without access to 3D outcomes, predictions to be locked before outcome evaluation, and performance to be tested across independently generated CRISPR datasets. Here, we developed target-specific, expression-based Elastic Net models for a fixed panel of 20 genes using 852 pan-cancer 2D models. Candidate molecular representations were compared using patient-grouped nested cross-validation. The selected model was evaluated once in a locked 2D holdout for internal confirmation conditional on historical target selection. Without outcome-guided retraining, the frozen model was then examined exploratorily in Broad organoids and subjected to blinded independent external validation in a non-overlapping Sanger organoid cohort. Target-level findings from the two 3D cohorts were integrated in a retrospective transportability synthesis, and the locked models were subsequently projected onto an independent pancreatic organoid RNA-seq cohort lacking measured CRISPR outcomes. The primary hypothesis concerned ranking organoids by measured dependency for each gene, not ranking genes within an individual organoid. We hypothesized that this between-organoid signal would remain detectable but attenuated in 3D systems. A separate retrospective analysis evaluated gene ranking within individual organoids using the same frozen predictions. This secondary analysis addressed a different prediction task and did not replace the original primary evaluation. 

Study design, data sources, and evidence hierarchy. Target-specific Elastic Net models were developed from transcriptomic profiles and CRISPR Gene Effect values in 852 two-dimensional cancer models obtained from DepMap Public 26Q1 and the CCLE. Candidate representations were compared in 681 development models, and the selected expression-only representation was evaluated once on a locked 171-model internal holdout set; the final models were then refitted on all 852 models, keeping the targets, target-specific features, hyperparameters, and primary metric fixed. Historical target selection included the holdout models, so this evaluation provides internal confirmation conditional on the selected panel. Without retraining, these models were applied to 94 Broad NextGen organoids for exploratory evaluation and to 148 Sanger organoids representing 144 donors for blinded independent external validation. Sanger predictions were sealed using the 256-bit Secure Hash Algorithm before access to CRISPR outcomes. Broad and Sanger target-level results were subsequently combined in a retrospective transportability synthesis. The Tiriac pancreatic RNA-sequencing cohort comprised 55 samples and was used only for secondary projection because measured genome-scale CRISPR dependency outcomes were unavailable. The dashed border identifies this non-validation application cohort. The figure depicts the primary workflow; the model specification and prespecified primary metric remained unchanged. The separate retrospective within-organoid ranking analysis is described in  Section 2.10.1  and  Tables S17–S19  and was not a new blinded validation. Colored boxes distinguish the five labeled stages, and arrows indicate their order in the workflow. CCLE, Cancer Cell Line Encyclopedia; CRISPR, clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats; DepMap, Cancer Dependency Map; RNA, ribonucleic acid; SHA-256, 256-bit Secure Hash Algorithm. 

As shown in  Figure 1 , the first layer comprised 2D model development and locking using DepMap Public 26Q1 and CCLE molecular data [ 3 , 4 , 13 , 14 ]. The analysis-ready population included 852 pan-cancer models with continuous CRISPR Gene Effect values for a fixed 20-gene target panel. Models were partitioned by PatientID, with ModelID used as a fallback only when PatientID was unavailable. This yielded 681 development models from 658 patient groups and 171 locked internal-holdout models from 165 patient groups. No PatientID was shared between partitions. Candidate molecular representations were compared exclusively within the development partition using patient-grouped, lineage-stratified 5 × 5 nested cross-validation. Before the locked-holdout outcomes were evaluated, the selected representation, target panel, target-specific feature identities, hyperparameters, primary metric, and prediction files were frozen. After the one-time holdout evaluation, the selected expression-only Elastic Net specification was refitted on all 852 models for downstream deployment. Only imputation, scaling, and coefficient parameters were re-estimated. The targets, features, hyperparameters, and primary metric remained fixed. 

The third layer used the Sanger tumor-derived organoid dependency map [ 39 ]. Following cross-resource identity auditing and conservative exclusion of Broad-linked and same-donor records, the final cohort contained 148 organoids from 144 donors. The fixed 20-target prediction matrix, cohort registry, protocol, and model and feature manifests were written and cryptographically sealed using the 256-bit Secure Hash Algorithm (SHA-256) before Sanger CRISPR outcomes were accessed. Sanger-148 was therefore treated as a one-time, outcome-blinded independent external-validation cohort, with donor structure retained in uncertainty estimation and sensitivity analyses. 

In the fourth layer, the Broad-94 and Sanger-148 target-level rank correlations were combined only after the two cohorts had been evaluated separately [ 38 , 39 ]. The retrospective transportability synthesis assessed directional concordance, pooled target-level associations, and between-cohort heterogeneity. It was not treated as a third external validation and did not alter any component of the frozen model. Because Broad/DepMap Chronos Gene Effect and Sanger corrected CRISPR log fold change (LFC) values are assay-specific scales, the synthesis focused on rank transfer rather than absolute numerical equivalence. 

The fifth layer applied the same frozen models to 55 RNA-seq samples from the Tiriac pancreatic organoid cohort, accessed through the Genomic Data Commons (GDC) ORGANOID-PANCREATIC project [ 32 ]. The resource comprised 44 tumor samples from 38 independent tumor cases and 11 normal samples from 11 independent normal cases. Because measured genome-scale CRISPR dependency outcomes were unavailable, this layer was restricted to secondary transcriptomic projection and expression-domain diagnostics and was not used to estimate predictive accuracy or therapeutic sensitivity. Across all layers, no 3D observation contributed to 2D candidate selection, feature learning, hyperparameter tuning, or deployment coefficient estimation. After access to 3D data, no retraining, recalibration, target or feature reselection, hyperparameter retuning, or replacement of the prespecified primary metric was permitted. The secondary within-organoid endpoints were evaluated retrospectively and did not modify the frozen predictions or original primary results. Detailed cohort construction, preprocessing, modeling, and statistical procedures are provided in  Section 2.2 ,  Section 2.3 ,  Section 2.4 ,  Section 2.5 ,  Section 2.6 ,  Section 2.7 ,  Section 2.8 ,  Section 2.9  and  Section 2.10 , and the archived code, frozen models, locked predictions, and verification records are described in  Section 2.11 . 

Using a locked 20% test fraction and global random seed 20260826, we divided the 852 models into 681 development models and 171 locked 2D holdout models, representing 658 and 165 unique patient groups, respectively. No PatientID overlapped between partitions. We used the development partition to compare candidate input representations, learn target-specific features, and select hyperparameters. Within the current model-development workflow, holdout outcomes were not accessed until the development decisions and prediction files had been locked. This restriction did not apply retrospectively to the earlier target-panel selection. 

As documented in  Table S1 , the panel had been defined in an earlier 2D workflow that used all 852 models. Consequently, the 171-model holdout was independent of the current candidate representation, feature selection, hyperparameters, and model-fitting decisions, but not fully independent of historical target panel selection. Its performance was therefore interpreted as internal confirmation conditional on the selected panel, not an unbiased estimate of generalization for the complete analysis pipeline. Independent 3D validation assessed new samples for the same preselected genes; it did not evaluate transferability to unselected genes or across the genome. 

The gene-expression block comprised 19,215 protein-coding genes, represented as log2-transformed transcripts per million (TPM) values [log2(TPM + 1)], with no subsequent logarithmic transformation. The copy-number block comprised 19,955 whole-genome sequencing-derived gene-level linear, ploidy-relative values. Somatic variants were encoded separately as binary indicators of damage-mutation and hotspot-mutation carriers for 19,626 genes. OncotreeLineage was encoded as 29 binary indicators. The source blocks, encodings, initial dimensions, and candidate representations are summarized in  Table S2 . 

As shown in  Table S2 , lineage-only used the 29 lineage indicators as a non-molecular reference. Four molecular candidates were ordered by increasing representational complexity: expression-only (19,215 initial features); expression + copy number (39,170); expression + mutation (58,467), combining expression with damaging and hotspot indicators; and all omics + lineage (78,451), combining all four molecular blocks with lineage. The lineage-only reference was not eligible under the molecular one-standard-error selection rule. 

Eligibility screening was relearned within every applicable training partition. Expression and copy-number features were excluded when the missing-value fraction in the training partition exceeded 0.20 or the variance was less than or equal to 1 × 10 −8 . The same variance threshold was applied to mutation indicators and to lineage indicators within the all-omics candidate set; all 29 indicators were retained for the lineage-only reference. For each target, nonfinite values were temporarily replaced with the training-partition means solely for univariate screening; the 300 eligible features with the largest absolute Pearson correlations were then retained. 

Model development and candidate-representation comparison were performed exclusively in the 681-model development partition. To separate model selection from performance estimation, we used a prespecified 5 outer × 5 inner patient-grouped, lineage-stratified nested cross-validation design [ 22 , 23 ]. PatientID was retained as the group identifier, with ModelID used only as a fallback when PatientID was unavailable. No patient group crossed a training-validation boundary. Within each split, OncotreeLineage categories represented by fewer than five models were collapsed into Other_Rare for stratification only; the original lineage labels remained unchanged for modeling and reporting. Each development model contributed to exactly one outer-test prediction. The full frozen validation and tuning protocol is provided in  Table S3 . 

Within each outer-training partition, a separate five-fold inner validation was constructed. The eligibility filters, target-specific top-300 feature screening, median imputation, and standardization described in  Section 2.3  were relearned in every inner-training fold and transferred unchanged to the corresponding inner-validation fold. Outer-test observations contributed to neither feature identities, preprocessing parameters, nor hyperparameter selection. Information flow was restricted to inner training, inner validation, hyperparameter selection, refitting on the complete outer-training partition, and one-time prediction of the outer-test partition. 

A separate Elastic Net regression model was fitted for each of the 20 targets and each molecular representation [ 21 ]. The frozen search space was l1_ratio = {0.10, 0.50, 0.90} and alpha = {0.001, 0.01, 0.1}; we used a maximum of 20,000 iterations, a convergence tolerance of 0.0001, and cyclic coordinate updates. The inner objective was the mean target-specific Spearman correlation across the five inner-validation folds. We prioritized settings that converged in all inner folds; among them, we selected the setting with the highest mean inner Spearman correlation, resolving remaining ties in favor of a larger alpha and then a lower l1_ratio. When no setting converged in all five folds, we selected based on the number of converged folds, mean inner Spearman correlation, and larger alpha. We refit the chosen setting on the complete outer-training partition and applied it once to the outer-test partition. For the lineage-only reference, target-specific ridge regression was fitted to the 29 lineage indicators, with alpha = {0.1, 1, 10, 100} compared in the inner validation. 

Predictions from the five outer-test folds were combined to generate out-of-fold (OOF) predictions for all 681 development models. The primary candidate score was the arithmetic mean of the 20 target-specific Spearman correlations, termed the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We retained target-specific mean absolute error (MAE), root mean squared error (RMSE), coefficient of determination (R 2 ), calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics, which did not alter model selection. Stability of the target-specific feature sets across outer folds was summarized using pairwise Jaccard similarity. 

Candidate selection used only the development OOF predictions. Uncertainty for each candidate was estimated from 2000 bootstrap resamples of PatientID clusters. The one-standard-error threshold was defined as the highest observed molecular-candidate OOF macro Spearman correlation minus the candidate’s bootstrap standard error. Among molecular candidates meeting this threshold, we selected the least complex representation according to the prespecified order: expression-only, expression + copy number, expression + mutation, and all omics + lineage. Lineage-only was a non-molecular reference and was excluded from this rule. 

After development selection had been locked, only the selected expression-only representation was applied to the 171-model 2D holdout. For each target, the 300 expression features were relearned in all 681 development models, and the final target-specific l1_ratio and alpha pair defined in  Section 2.4  were fixed. Median imputation, standardization, and Elastic Net coefficients were fitted using the 681 development models and applied unchanged to the 171 holdout models. Predictions for the 20 targets were written together with ModelID, PatientID, and OncotreeLineage. Before the observed holdout CRISPR Gene Effect rows were indexed for evaluation, the prediction files, model manifest, and selected-feature registry were sealed with SHA-256 digests. The one-time holdout evaluation and deployment-model finalization protocol is summarized in  Table S4 . 

The locked holdout outcomes were evaluated once after the prediction lock. The primary endpoint was the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We calculated target-specific Spearman correlation, MAE, RMSE, R 2 , calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics. We estimated uncertainty in the macro Spearman correlation from 2000 bootstrap resamples of PatientID clusters; we retained all rows belonging to a sampled patient group together, and the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles defined the 95% confidence interval (CI). The holdout-minus-development difference distribution was obtained by subtracting the development OOF bootstrap value from the holdout bootstrap value within each iteration. No model, feature, hyperparameter, or metric was changed during evaluation. Because historical target-panel selection used all 852 models, this stage provided internal confirmation conditional on that selection. We did not interpret its performance as an unbiased generalization estimate. The patient-cluster bootstrap did not account for uncertainty or optimism arising from historical target selection. 

To evaluate information beyond tumor lineage, the lineage-only ridge reference described in  Section 2.4  was refitted in the 681 development models and applied to the same 171 holdout models. The difference in macro Spearman correlation between the selected model and the lineage-only reference was estimated using common PatientID-cluster bootstrap resamples. When more than one model was associated with a patient, observed outcomes and predictions were additionally averaged at the PatientID level. With predictions fixed, complete 20-target outcome vectors were permuted among patient groups within each OncotreeLineage stratum for 2000 iterations. The one-sided empirical  p -value was calculated as (1 + the number of null statistics greater than or equal to the observed statistic)/2001. The patient-aggregated macro Spearman correlation was retained as a sensitivity estimate. 

After completing the locked 2D evaluation, we refitted the final model authorized for 3D application to all 852 models. The expression-only representation, fixed 20-target panel, 300 target-specific feature identities, target-specific l1_ratio and alpha settings, and primary metric were not reopened. We re-estimated only the median-imputation values, standardization parameters, and Elastic Net coefficients. Within the current model-fitting workflow, holdout outcomes entered coefficient estimation only in this final all-852 fit, after the internal performance estimate had been fixed. They were not used to revise candidate selection, targets, features, hyperparameters, or the reported 681-to-171 evaluation. A separate serialized bundle was recorded for each target, including feature identities and types, the imputer, scaler, fitted estimator, training-sample count, model role, and protocol hash. Nonconverged deployment fits were not accepted. 

As documented in  Table S4 , the locked internal-evaluation models and the all-852 deployment models were analytically distinct. The former supported only the 681-to-171 performance estimate; the latter constituted the final models applied without retraining to the 3D cohorts. The deployment-model manifest, selected-feature registry, target-specific model bundles, and final lock records were SHA-256 sealed before access to any new 3D data. Subsequent 3D applications prohibited retraining, recalibration, target or feature reselection, hyperparameter retuning, and metric switching; blind predictions had to be written and cryptographically sealed before outcome merging. 

The expression-only deployment model, finalized in  Section 2.5  and refitted to all 852 2D models, was applied without Broad-specific retraining. For each target, the 300 locked expression features were matched to the Broad expression matrix using Entrez Gene identifiers and ordered exactly as stored in the corresponding model bundle. Feature coverage was defined as the proportion of the 300 frozen predictors present in the external expression matrix for each target. The prespecified 80% minimum (at least 240 of 300 predictors) was a pragmatic input-eligibility rule that limited unavailable predictors to 20% while allowing incomplete gene coverage across data resources. The cutoff was not optimized against external predictive performance and does not establish reliable prediction at 20% missingness. All 300 features were available for every Broad target, yielding 100% target-specific coverage. We applied the 2D-fitted median-imputation values, standardization parameters, Elastic Net coefficients, and l1_ratio and alpha settings unchanged. No Broad-fitted imputation, scaling, feature selection, coefficient adaptation, or target substitution was performed. This procedure generated a finite 94-organoid × 20-target prediction matrix. 

The primary estimand was the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We calculated target-specific Spearman correlation, MAE, RMSE, R 2 , calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics and did not use them to reopen model selection. We estimated macro- and target-specific uncertainty from 5000 model-level bootstrap resamples performed with replacement within OncotreeLineage strata, preserving the observed lineage counts; 95% CIs were defined by the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. The lineage-only ridge reference was applied to the same 94 organoids, and the expression-only-minus-lineage difference was estimated using paired bootstrap resamples. Target-specific partial Spearman correlations, adjusted for lineage indicators; lineage subgroup estimates for groups containing at least 7 models; and leave-one-lineage-out analyses were retained as secondary sensitivity analyses. 

For the panel-wide permutation test, predictions were held fixed, and each organoid’s complete 20-target outcome vector was permuted among model rows within the same OncotreeLineage stratum for 5000 iterations, thereby preserving cross-target dependence and lineage composition. The one-sided empirical  p -value was calculated as (1 + the number of null macro Spearman statistics greater than or equal to the observed statistic)/5001. Target-specific permutation  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery rate (FDR) procedure. All Broad resampling procedures used random seed 20260829. As specified in  Table S5 , rank transportability was the primary endpoint. Absolute-scale error, calibration, and lineage sensitivity were secondary diagnostics. Broad results were not used to retrain the model or select targets based on effect direction or statistical significance. 

The independent Sanger 3D validation cohort was constructed from the RNA-seq and CRISPR-screening records of the tumor-derived organoid dependency map [ 39 ]. Before any Sanger target outcome was accessed, 162 organoids from 157 unique donors with both RNA-seq profiles and quality-controlled CRISPR-screening records were screened for eligibility. To prevent reuse of samples represented in the Broad exploratory layer or elsewhere in the Broad NextGen resource, Sanger sample and donor identifiers were cross-referenced against the Broad mapping records and the Broad-94 registry [ 38 , 39 ]. Thirteen Broad-linked organoids and one additional organoid from the same donor as a Broad-linked model were conservatively excluded. The final cohort therefore comprised 148 organoids from 144 unique donors, with no exact Broad-94 identity or identifiable same-donor Broad overlap. Cohort construction, harmonization, and the outcome-blinded lock procedure are summarized in  Table S6 . 

The final cohort included five tumor types: 81 colorectal organoids from 79 donors, 49 esophageal organoids from 49 donors, 11 ovarian organoids from 9 donors, 4 pancreatic organoids from 4 donors, and 3 gastric organoids from 3 donors. RNA-seq input and experimental CRISPR coverage for all 20 locked targets were available for every included organoid. Colorectal and esophageal subgroup analyses were prespecified as secondary, the ovarian subgroup was exploratory, and the pancreatic and gastric subgroups were treated as descriptive because of their small sample sizes. The primary external-validation analysis used all 148 organoids, with donor structure preserved in uncertainty estimation and sensitivity analyses. 

Before the Sanger CRISPR outcomes were opened, the 148-organoid × 20-target blinded prediction matrix, cohort registry, protocol, model manifest, and feature manifest were written and sealed with SHA-256 digests; all 11 prespecified seal checks passed. Only after this lock was established were the Sanger outcomes accessed once and merged with the predictions in the frozen sample and target order, yielding 2960 finite prediction-outcome pairs. The external outcome was the corrected and scaled gene-level CRISPR LFC, with more negative values indicating stronger dependency. For the 13 organoids screened with two CRISPR libraries, the official Sanger covariate was applied: minLib was selected when the code was 1 and v1_1 when the code was 0. The available quality-controlled screen was used for single-library organoids. This externally defined selection rule was fixed before inspection of the target outcomes. 

The primary external-validation estimand was the target-specific Spearman correlation between the blinded predictions and Sanger LFC values, followed by the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). This primary estimand evaluates the ordering of organoids for a fixed gene and does not assess the ordering of genes within an organoid. Rank correlation was selected because the Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values are on assay-specific scales and were not assumed to be numerically comparable. Uncertainty was estimated with 5000 donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, retaining all organoids from a sampled donor together; 95% CIs were obtained from the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. For the null distribution, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, predictions were held fixed, and each donor-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was permuted within tumor-type/lineage strata for 5000 iterations. The two-sided empirical  p -value was calculated as (1 + the number of absolute null macro correlations at least as large as the observed absolute macro correlation)/5001. Target-specific permutation  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. 

A lineage-only comparator, frozen before access to Sanger outcomes and based on patient-weighted 2D lineage means, was applied to the same cohort. We evaluated the expression-only-minus-lineage macro difference using paired donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, and we also calculated partial Spearman correlations adjusted for the primary tumor-type indicators. The influence of repeated donors was examined in two additional analyses: one retained the lexicographically first sample identifier per donor, and the other averaged predictions and outcomes within donor. An independent replay recomputed cohort size, donor count, overlap exclusions, feature coverage, target and prediction order, the complete 2960-pair grid, correlation statistics, bootstrap and permutation results, lineage adjustment, and file hashes; all 22 checks passed. As detailed in  Table S6 , no model, target, feature, hyperparameter, or prespecified primary metric was changed after outcome unlock. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints were evaluated separately using the unchanged predictions. 

The target-specific results from the Broad-94 exploratory evaluation and the Sanger-148 blinded independent external validation were combined retrospectively only after the two cohort-specific analyses had been completed [ 38 , 39 ]. The fixed 20-target panel defined in  Section 2.2  was aligned using Entrez Gene identifiers, and no targets were removed based on effect direction, magnitude, or statistical significance. Because Broad used Chronos Gene Effect values and Sanger used corrected CRISPR LFC values, the synthesis addressed target-specific rank transportability rather than absolute-scale equivalence. The Tiriac pancreatic projection was excluded from this synthesis because it did not contain measured genome-scale CRISPR outcomes. The synthesis protocol is summarized in  Table S7 . 

Directional concordance was defined as a positive Spearman correlation for the same target in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148. The number of common-positive targets was compared with a one-sided exact binomial distribution with  n  = 20 and a null success probability of 0.50. This comparison was interpreted only as a descriptive sign benchmark: dependence among targets limits the binomial interpretation, and the synthesis was defined after the cohort-specific results were known. Agreement in relative target performance was evaluated separately using the two-sided Spearman correlation between the vectors of 20 Broad and 20 Sanger target-specific correlations. 

For target-level pooling, each cohort-specific Spearman correlation was transformed to the Fisher-z scale. We derived inverse-variance weights from the cohort-specific uncertainty intervals obtained from the lineage-stratified Broad bootstrap and the donor-clustered Sanger bootstrap. Because Broad donor structure was incompletely ascertained, its original uncertainty weights may overstate precision; the synthesis is conditional on this unresolved model-level independence assumption. A fixed-effects pooled estimate was calculated for each target and then transformed back to the Spearman correlation scale. The resulting two-sided meta-analytic  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. Because only two 3D cohorts were available, random-effects estimates and the I 2  heterogeneity statistic were retained as descriptive sensitivity measures and were not used as confirmatory decision criteria. 

Within each cohort, we subtracted the target-specific lineage-only correlation from the corresponding expression-only correlation. We used a one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the 20 paired differences to assess the incremental contribution of expression beyond lineage. A retrospective evidence classification was used for reporting only. Tier A required a positive correlation in both cohorts, a meta-analytic FDR q < 0.05, and target-level FDR q < 0.05 in at least one individual cohort. Tier B indicated positive correlations in both cohorts without fulfillment of all Tier A criteria. Tier C indicated a non-positive correlation in at least one cohort. As specified in  Table S7 , the evidence tiers did not alter the locked panel or any model component, and no retraining, target or feature reselection, hyperparameter tuning, metric switching, or direction-based target removal was permitted during the synthesis. 

After completion of 2D model development, Broad-94 exploration, Sanger-148 blinded validation, and the retrospective cross-cohort synthesis, the frozen expression-only deployment models were applied to the Tiriac/ORGANOID-PANCREATIC cohort as a secondary biological projection [ 32 ]. Because measured genome-scale CRISPR dependency outcomes were unavailable, this analysis was prespecified as an application to an independent pancreatic transcriptome rather than external validation; predictive accuracy and CRISPR performance were not estimated. Fifty-five publicly available RNA-seq Spliced Transcripts Alignment to a Reference (STAR) count files from the GDC project were obtained from the official public Amazon Simple Storage Service mirror. The cohort comprised 44 tumor samples representing 38 independent tumor cases and 11 samples from 11 independent normal cases; group membership was defined from the source TissueType metadata. Cohort preparation and projection procedures are summarized in  Table S8 . 

The tpm_unstranded field in the STAR output was used as the abundance measure. Rows mapping to the same gene symbol were summed at the TPM level before log2(TPM + 1) transformation. The resulting sample-by-feature matrix was aligned to the locked expression features of the 20 target-specific Elastic Net deployment bundles finalized in all 852 2D models. The target panel, target-specific feature identities, stored imputation and standardization objects, hyperparameters, coefficients, and projection definitions were applied without modification. No Tiriac sample contributed to model fitting, feature selection, recalibration, retuning, or endpoint definition. 

The 20-target median projection vector for Tiriac tumor cases was compared separately with the corresponding median prediction vectors for Broad-94 and Sanger-148. This comparison assessed whether the projected pancreatic target hierarchy was consistent across organoid applications. Concordance was quantified using two-sided Spearman correlations across the 20 targets. Because all compared values were predictions from the same locked model family and Tiriac lacked measured CRISPR outcomes, these correlations were interpreted as secondary evidence of target-ranking consistency rather than independent evidence of predictive accuracy. 

Expression-domain shift was evaluated across the 3790 locked expression features that are jointly available in Tiriac, Broad-94, and Sanger-148. Multivariable separation among cohorts was visualized by principal component analysis (PCA). For Tiriac versus Broad-94 and Tiriac versus Sanger-148, a standardized mean difference (SMD) was calculated for each common feature, and shift magnitude was summarized using the median absolute SMD and the proportions of features with |SMD| > 1 and |SMD| > 2. The PCA and SMD diagnostics were descriptive and were not used to recalibrate the models, delete features, remove targets, or revise projections.  Table S8  defines the corresponding claim boundary: Tiriac results were described as projections or prioritizations and not as validated dependencies, confirmed dependencies, or therapeutic sensitivity. 

Statistical analyses preserved the prespecified role and strongest available biological independence unit of each study layer. In the development, locked 2D holdout, Broad-94, and Sanger-148 analyses, the primary rank-based measure was the Spearman correlation between predicted and observed outcomes for each target and the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). MAE, RMSE, R 2 , calibration intercept, and calibration slope were secondary diagnostics in the development, locked-holdout, and Broad analyses and did not reopen model selection. Because Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values were not treated as numerically equivalent, the Sanger validation and cross-cohort synthesis were based on rank correlation rather than absolute-scale error. Target-wise correlation operates across organoids for each gene; within-organoid correlation instead operates across the 20 genes for each organoid. Averaging target-wise correlations does not test within-organoid gene ranking. The original analysis hierarchy, inferential units, resampling procedures, and multiplicity rules are summarized in  Table S9 ; the retrospective within-organoid endpoints are defined in  Section 2.10.1  and  Tables S17–S19 . 

Resampling units followed the available biological grouping. Development and locked 2D holdout uncertainty used 2000 PatientID-cluster bootstrap replicates, retaining all models from a sampled patient group together. Broad-94 used 5000 model-level bootstrap replicates within OncotreeLineage strata because PatientID was unavailable in the original evaluation records; the later partial-donor sensitivities are reported separately in  Section 2.10.3 . Sanger-148 used 5000 donor-cluster bootstrap replicates, retaining all organoids from a sampled donor together. Tiriac tumor-normal comparisons used 5000 case-level bootstrap replicates within the 38-tumor and 11-normal groups. Unless otherwise specified, 95% percentile CIs were defined by the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. Paired model comparisons used common resampling indices. The standard error used in the development one-standard-error rule was derived from the 2000-replicate PatientID-cluster bootstrap distribution of OOF predictions. 

For permutation analyses, predictions remained fixed and complete 20-target observed-outcome vectors were moved without disrupting the prespecified biological grouping. The locked 2D sensitivity analysis used 2000 one-sided permutations of patient-aggregated outcome vectors within OncotreeLineage strata. Broad-94 used 5000 one-sided permutations of ModelID-level outcome vectors within lineage. Sanger-148 used 5000 two-sided permutations after donor averaging, with donor-level outcome vectors moved within tumor-type/lineage strata and the absolute macro correlation used as the test statistic. Empirical  p -values used the standard plus-one numerator and denominator correction. Broad and Sanger target-specific permutation  p -values were adjusted separately within their 20-target families using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. 

In the retrospective cross-cohort synthesis, common-positive direction was assessed with a one-sided exact binomial benchmark ( n  = 20; null probability = 0.50), interpreted descriptively because targets were correlated and the synthesis was retrospective. Target-specific correlations were pooled on the Fisher-z scale using inverse-variance fixed-effect weights derived from cohort-specific bootstrap uncertainty, and the 20 two-sided meta-analytic  p -values were adjusted using the Benjamini–Hochberg procedure. Random-effects estimates and I 2  were descriptive because only two cohorts were available. Target-panel sensitivity used 20,000 target-bootstrap resamples, while cross-target dependence was assessed using 50,000 one-sided simultaneous block permutations at the Broad ModelID and Sanger donor-mean levels. Expression-only-minus-lineage target differences were evaluated separately in Broad and Sanger using one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank tests. None of these retrospective analyses was interpreted as a new validation cohort. 

Tests were two-sided unless explicitly prespecified as one-sided. Within each stated 20-target family, FDR q < 0.05 defined the multiplicity-adjusted target-level significance; exact  p -values, effect sizes, and CIs were reported together where applicable. Exploratory and post hoc analyses retained those labels even when a statistical threshold was crossed, and no analysis result changed the target panel, feature identities, hyperparameters, primary metrics, or previously generated predictions. Analyses were implemented in Python 3.12.13 using NumPy 2.3.5, pandas 2.2.3, SciPy 1.17.0, scikit-learn 1.8.0, and joblib 1.5.3. Random seeds, environment files, protocols, SHA-256 manifests, and integrity checks were preserved in the archived reproducibility package. 

A retrospective secondary analysis evaluated whether the frozen expression-only predictions ranked the 20 genes within individual organoids in agreement with measured CRISPR outcomes. This secondary analysis was defined after the external outcomes were available and was not a new blinded validation. It retained all 20 targets and all 94 Broad and 148 Sanger organoids; Tiriac was excluded because measured dependency outcomes were unavailable. No predictions, models, features, hyperparameters, or targets were changed, and the original target-wise primary analysis was retained. 

For each organoid, we calculated Spearman correlation across its 20 predicted and 20 observed scores, using average ranks for ties, and summarized the correlations by their arithmetic mean. Lower scores denoted stronger measured or predicted dependency. The main comparator assigned every organoid the same per-gene mean of the observed outcomes across all 852 2D training models, with each training model weighted equally. This fixed ranking tested whether expression predictions improved on gene-level average dependency. We also reused the archived lineage-only predictions: the ridge reference in Broad and patient-weighted 2D lineage means in Sanger. No external outcomes were used to fit either comparator ( Table S17 ). 

To examine a short candidate list, we calculated the overlap between the three lowest predicted scores and the three lowest measured scores, divided by three, within each organoid. The cohort mean was expressed as a percentage. Ties at the selection boundary were to be resolved by the fixed target identifiers; none occurred. Expression-minus-comparator differences were calculated within the same organoids for both the full-ranking correlation and top-three overlap ( Table S18 ). These endpoints assess agreement with measured CRISPR score ordering within the selected panel, not cancer selectivity, cross-gene calibration, drug response, or clinical benefit. 

We estimated 95% percentile CIs using 10,000 paired bootstrap resamples: organoid models sampled within observed lineage strata for Broad, and donor clusters sampled with all their organoids retained together for Sanger. The Sanger main estimate remained organoid-weighted. A donor-level sensitivity analysis first averaged the predicted and observed 20-gene vectors within each of 144 donors, then recalculated both endpoints with equal donor weights and a 10,000-replicate donor bootstrap ( Table S19 ). Seeds were 20260928, 20260929, and 20260930 for Broad, Sanger, and donor-mean sensitivity, respectively. All correlations were defined. Intervals were conditional on the frozen models and fixed panel; they did not include model-training or gene-panel selection uncertainty. The secondary comparisons were not adjusted for multiplicity and were interpreted as exploratory. 

Using the same frozen models, we compared training-median imputation with two alternatives: zero on the log2(TPM + 1) scale before standardization, and the stored training-reference mean for each feature. These means were estimated after median imputation in the 852-model training data and correspond to zero standardized input, up to floating-point rounding. Only absent features were replaced; observed expression values, including measured zeros, were retained. We summarized absolute projection changes, Spearman correlation across the 20 genes within each profile, and top-three set overlap relative to the median-imputed reference. We evaluated sample-level results and case-averaged profiles, including 38 tumor and 11 normal cases. We also repeated the original tumor–normal comparisons using the same 5000 bootstrap resamples (seed 20260830), Mann–Whitney tests, and 20-target FDR adjustment. These are descriptive checks of projection stability, not prediction accuracy. Because the models are linear and the missing-feature pattern is shared, constant replacement values create a common offset per target; preservation of within-target sample ranks and tumor–normal differences is therefore expected algebraically ( Tables S20 and S21 ). 

A retrospective metadata audit linked 16 of the 94 models to 15 PatientID values in the supplied DepMap Model.csv and nine additional models to nine source-specific donor identifiers in the archived Sanger exclusion registry. Thus, donor information was available for 25 models, forming 24 source-specific groups, while 69 models remained unresolved. ACH-002883 and ACH-002884 shared PatientID PT-N65Hvq. The DepMap and Sanger donor namespaces could not be fully reconciled, so the 24 groups do not establish 24 independently verified biological donors. No donor identity was inferred from lineage or model-name similarity ( Table S22 ). 

Retrospective sensitivity analyses used the unchanged predictions, outcomes, and 20-gene panel. Each member of the known same-donor pair was omitted in turn. A conditional full-cohort bootstrap grouped the known pair and treated unresolved models as singleton units, yielding 93 resampling groups; this assumption does not establish independence of the 69 unresolved models. In the donor-documented subset, model-level and donor-cluster bootstraps were compared on the same 25 models, and a separate analysis averaged prediction, outcome, and lineage-reference vectors within the 24 source-specific donor groups before evaluation. All analyses used 5000 paired bootstrap replicates within the observed OncotreeLineage strata (seed 20260929), retaining every model from each sampled group. Percentile 95% intervals were calculated at 2.5% and 97.5%. Target-wise correlations were reranked within each resample and averaged across all 20 targets; an undefined target correlation contributed zero, following the archived convention. Organoid weighting was retained except in the donor-mean analysis. Full-cohort and known-subset model-level replays with the same seed provide matched references; they do not replace the original CIs.  Tables S23 and S24  report the primary target-wise and secondary within-organoid endpoints. These post hoc intervals were not adjusted for multiple sensitivity comparisons, and no new confirmatory permutation or FDR claim was made. 

The study’s reproducibility package is available on Zenodo ( https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22472622 ). The package includes custom analysis and figure-generation scripts; frozen cohort, split, target, and feature registries; trained model artifacts; locked predictions; analysis-ready inputs and processed outputs; bootstrap and permutation results; figure source data; software-environment records; and SHA-256 integrity manifests. It also contains the code, inputs, and results for the primary analyses, retrospective within-organoid gene ranking, Tiriac missing-feature imputation sensitivity, and Broad cohort-size and donor sensitivity analyses. Complete original third-party data releases remain available from the repositories identified in the Data Availability Statement. 

Five input representations—the lineage-only reference and four molecular candidates—were compared in the development partition of 681 models representing 658 patient groups, using identical patient-grouped outer folds. Candidate-level OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlations and paired differences relative to expression-only are shown in  Figure 2 a,b; full candidate- and target-level results are provided in  Table S10 . 

Development-cohort omics ablation and selection of the expression-only model. ( a ) Patient-grouped OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlations for the lineage-only reference and four molecular candidate representations in the 681-model development cohort. Error bars show 95% percentile confidence intervals from 2000 PatientID-cluster bootstrap replicates. The blue square denotes the expression-only candidate selected by the prespecified one-standard-error rule. ( b ) Paired differences in macro Spearman correlation between each augmented molecular representation and expression-only. As shown in  Figure 2 a, the highest observed OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlation was obtained with expression + copy number (ρ = 0.4081; 95% CI, 0.3894–0.4243). Performance was nearly identical for expression-only (ρ = 0.4071; 95% CI, 0.3893–0.4232), expression + mutation (ρ = 0.4073; 95% CI, 0.3908–0.4235), and all omics + lineage (ρ = 0.4031; 95% CI, 0.3864–0.4184). The lineage-only reference produced a lower macro correlation (ρ = 0.2598; 95% CI, 0.2388–0.2806) and, as prespecified, was not eligible for molecular candidate selection. 

Relative to expression-only, paired PatientID-cluster bootstrap differences were +0.0010 (95% CI, −0.0047 to 0.0066) for expression + copy number, +0.0002 (95% CI, −0.0075 to 0.0079) for expression + mutation, and −0.0039 (95% CI, −0.0123 to 0.0043) for all omics + lineage ( Figure 2 b). All three intervals included zero, providing no evidence that adding copy-number, mutation, or combined omic and lineage information produced a reliable improvement in development performance. 

After model selection was locked, the expression-only model, target-specific 300-feature sets, and final Elastic Net hyperparameters were applied once to the locked 2D holdout of 171 models representing 165 patient groups. Panel-wide, paired-contrast, target-level, and deployment-finalization results are shown in  Figure 3 a–d; detailed numerical results are provided in  Table S11 . 

Locked two-dimensional holdout performance and deployment-model finalization. ( a ) Development OOF, locked two-dimensional holdout, and lineage-only holdout macro target-wise Spearman correlations. ( b ) Prespecified paired contrasts. Error bars in ( a , b ) show 95% intervals from 2000 PatientID-cluster bootstrap replicates. ( c ) Target-specific locked-holdout correlations, ordered from lowest to highest; all 20 were positive. ( d ) Finalization sequence. After the one-time holdout evaluation, the winning model was refitted on all 852 two-dimensional models while keeping the target panel, target-specific feature identities, hyperparameters, and primary metric fixed; all 20 deployment models converged before three-dimensional use. The target panel had been selected using the full source population, so holdout performance represents internal confirmation conditional on that selection, not an unbiased generalization estimate. As shown in  Figure 3 a,b, the development OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlation was 0.4071 (95% CI, 0.3893–0.4232), and the locked-holdout value was 0.4094 (95% CI, 0.3730–0.4383). The holdout-minus-development difference was +0.0023 (95% CI, −0.0372 to 0.0361). The interval included zero, indicating no detectable difference within this preselected panel. This comparison does not remove the dependence introduced by historical target selection. 

The lineage-only holdout reference yielded a macro correlation of 0.2969 (95% CI, 0.2529–0.3368). The expression-only-minus-lineage-only difference was +0.1125 (95% CI, 0.0765–0.1472). The patient-aggregated macro correlation was 0.4133, and the within-lineage permutation analysis across 165 patient groups yielded  p  = 0.00050. These prespecified checks supported rank information beyond lineage composition within the preselected panel. They do not establish independence from historical target selection. 

Because the historical target panel had been selected using all 852 2D models, these holdout results provide internal confirmation conditional on that selection. They do not provide an unbiased estimate of generalization for the complete analysis pipeline. The independent 3D validation assessed transfer to new organoid samples only for the same preselected panel. 

The finalized expression-only deployment model was applied to 94 Broad NextGen organoids without retraining, cohort-specific feature selection, or recalibration. All 300 locked expression features were available for every target, yielding 100% feature coverage. The 94 × 20 prediction matrix was written and SHA-256-sealed before alignment with the observed 3D Gene Effect outcomes. Because Broad-94 had been accessible in earlier methodological work, this analysis remained an exploratory 3D transfer assessment rather than an independent external validation. Panel-wide, target-level, bootstrap, and permutation results are shown in  Figure 4 a–d; full results and lineage sensitivity analyses are provided in  Table S12 .

As shown in  Figure 4 a,c,d, the macro target-wise Spearman correlation in Broad-94 was 0.1518 (95% lineage-stratified bootstrap CI, 0.1019–0.2006). Eighteen of 20 target correlations were positive, and the median target correlation was 0.1628. The Broad-94 macro correlation was 0.2576 lower than the locked 2D holdout value. In the within-lineage permutation analysis, an association at least as large as the observed value occurred rarely (one-sided  p  = 0.00080), supporting partial but attenuated transfer of rank information. 

The lineage-adjusted macro Spearman correlation was 0.1080, whereas the lineage-only reference yielded 0.0951. The paired expression-only-minus-lineage-only difference was +0.0567 (95% CI, 0.0040–0.1106). Thus, lineage composition did not fully explain the exploratory association, although it accounted for part of the observed signal. 

Among lineage groups containing at least seven models, macro correlations ranged from −0.0099 to 0.2786. Leave-one-lineage-out estimates ranged from 0.1202 to 0.1832, and the pooled association remained positive after every lineage exclusion. The original estimates used model-level resampling. The subsequent donor audit and sensitivity analyses below address the identified shared donor, while retaining uncertainty about unresolved donor relationships. 

The donor audit identified one confirmed same-donor pair among 25 models with donor information; 69 models remained unresolved ( Table S22 ). Omitting ACH-002883 or ACH-002884 gave macro target-wise correlations of 0.1516 and 0.1539, respectively, compared with the original 0.1518. Under the conditional full-cohort bootstrap, the expression-only estimate was 0.1518 (0.1009 to 0.2007) and its paired difference from lineage-only prediction was 0.0567 (0.0027 to 0.1081) (estimate and 95% CI;  Table S23 ). These findings quantify sensitivity to the identified pair, conditional on treating the unresolved models as separate units. 

In the 25-model donor-documented subset, the expression-only macro correlation was 0.0741 (−0.0154 to 0.1618), with an expression-minus-lineage difference of −0.0571 (−0.1502 to 0.0305) under source-specific donor-cluster resampling. Both intervals included zero, so this subset did not establish superiority over lineage. Averaging the vectors within the 24 source-specific donor groups gave 0.0727 (−0.0195 to 0.1629) for expression-only prediction. This subset is not a representative replacement for Broad-94; changes relative to the full cohort can reflect sample and lineage composition as well as grouping. Paired within-subset model-versus-cluster intervals and secondary ranking sensitivities are provided in  Tables S23 and S24 . Incomplete donor ascertainment prevents these analyses from verifying full-cohort independence or providing a fully donor-adjusted effective sample size. 

The outcome-blinded Sanger eligibility pool comprised 162 organoids from 157 donors with both RNA-seq profiles and quality-controlled CRISPR screening records. After excluding 13 Broad-linked organoids and one additional organoid from the same donor as a Broad-linked model, the independent validation cohort contained 148 organoids from 144 unique donors. Of the 3845 unique locked expression features used across the 20 target models, 3790 were measured in the Sanger matrix; target-specific coverage ranged from 291 to 300 of 300 features (97.0–100%). The 148 × 20 blind prediction matrix and the associated manifests were SHA-256 sealed before CRISPR LFC outcomes were accessed, and all 11 prespecified pre-outcome seal checks passed. Panel-wide validation, target-level results, and robustness analyses are summarized in  Figure 5  and  Table S13 .

As shown in  Figure 5 a,b, the frozen expression-only model achieved a macro target-wise Spearman correlation of 0.1015 in the independent Sanger-148 cohort (95% CI, 0.0637–0.1357). Seventeen of the 20 target estimates were positive. After repeated organoids were averaged within donors, 5000 two-sided permutations of complete 20-target outcome vectors within tumor-type strata yielded  p  = 0.00040. Thus, the locked 2D model carried a detectable rank-ordering signal into the independent 3D cohort, although the effect size was modest. 

As shown in  Figure 5 c and  Table S13 , the largest target-specific correlations were observed for  CDK6  (ρ = 0.3221; 95% CI, 0.1666–0.4702),  MDM4  (ρ = 0.2992; 95% CI, 0.1483–0.4410),  KEAP1  (ρ = 0.2967; 95% CI, 0.1256–0.4464),  CTNNB1  (ρ = 0.2831; 95% CI, 0.1031–0.4563), and  CCNE1  (ρ = 0.2792; 95% CI, 0.1014–0.4493). After Benjamini–Hochberg correction of the 20 target-level permutation  p -values, only  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1  met q < 0.05 (q = 0.0040 for each).  CCND3 ,  EGFR , and  NFE2L2  were the three targets with negative correlations; no targets were removed based on direction or significance. 

The tumor-type-adjusted macro Spearman correlation was 0.0882, whereas the frozen lineage-only reference achieved 0.0553 (95% CI, 0.0168–0.0917). The expression-only-minus-lineage difference was +0.0462, but its paired donor-cluster bootstrap interval included zero (95% CI, −0.0065 to 0.0986). The primary target-wise endpoint therefore did not establish superiority beyond tumor-origin information. The one-model-per-donor estimate (ρ = 0.1014) and donor-mean estimate (ρ = 0.0990) closely matched the primary result. Tumor-type subgroup estimates were 0.1030 for colorectal, 0.0654 for esophageal, 0.1755 for ovarian, 0.0300 for pancreatic, and 0.1500 for gastric organoids ( Figure 5 d). Ovarian results were exploratory, whereas pancreatic and gastric estimates were descriptive because of their small sample sizes. 

An independent replay reproduced the cohort composition, target and prediction order, feature coverage, primary and sensitivity statistics, lock records, and file hashes, with all 22 predefined checks passing. No model retraining, target or feature selection, hyperparameter adjustment, or change to the prespecified primary metric occurred after the Sanger outcomes were opened. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints are reported separately in  Section 3.7 . These findings support a statistically detectable but quantitatively modest independent 3D rank-transfer signal, with the strongest target-level support for  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1 . 

After the Broad-94 exploratory analysis and the Sanger-148 blinded external validation had been completed separately, their target-specific results were retrospectively combined for the unchanged 20-gene panel. The synthesis evaluated the direction and magnitude of rank transfer rather than the absolute agreement between the Broad Chronos Gene Effect and Sanger-corrected CRISPR LFC scales. Panel-wide and target-level synthesis results are summarized in  Figure 6  and  Table S14 . 

Retrospective cross-cohort transportability synthesis across Broad-94 and Sanger-148 organoids. ( b ) Target-wise Broad-94 versus Sanger-148 correlations, classified as Tier A, B, or C. ( c ) Fixed-effect target-level meta-analytic correlations with 95% confidence intervals. ( d ) Evidence-tier counts and sensitivity summaries. The exact-binomial benchmark was descriptive, and the 50,000-iteration correlation-aware permutation was post hoc; neither constituted a new external validation. In ( a ), gray, green, purple, and brown points and error bars denote the locked 2D holdout, Broad-94, Sanger-148, and combined 3D estimates, respectively.
