Fifteen of the 20 frozen targets had positive correlations in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148 (75%). A one-sided exact binomial comparison against a null success probability of 0.50 yielded  p  = 0.0207. The targets were correlated, and we defined the synthesis after the cohort-specific results were known. We therefore interpreted this value only as a descriptive benchmark. In the post hoc correlation-aware sensitivity analysis, Sanger outcomes were first averaged within donors, and each unit’s complete 20-target outcome vector was permuted as a block. Fourteen of 20 targets remained positive in both cohorts, and 50,000 simultaneous permutations yielded a one-sided empirical  p  = 1.0 × 10 −4  ( Figure 6 d). 

The correlation between the vectors of target-specific Broad and Sanger performance estimates was ρ = 0.2782 (two-sided  p  = 0.2350), indicating that panel-wide directional replication did not imply stable relative performance ordering across cohorts ( Figure 6 b). The expression-only-minus-lineage differences were positive at the panel level in Broad-94 (one-sided Wilcoxon  p  = 0.0379) but did not reach the corresponding threshold in Sanger-148 ( p  = 0.1012). Thus, the retrospective synthesis strengthened evidence for limited cross-cohort directionality but did not constitute a third external validation or support equivalent target performance across the two organoid resources. 

After completion of 2D model development, Broad-94 exploration, Sanger-148 validation, and retrospective synthesis, the same frozen expression-only deployment models were applied to the independent Tiriac pancreatic organoid RNA-seq cohort for secondary biological projection. The analysis included 55 samples: 44 tumor samples representing 38 independent tumor cases and 11 normal samples representing 11 independent normal cases. Of 3845 unique locked expression features, 3806 were observed (98.99%); target-specific coverage ranged from 292 to 300 of 300 features (97.33–100%), and all targets passed the prespecified 80% coverage gate. Because no measured genome-scale CRISPR dependency outcomes were available, these analyses evaluated model-output distributions and expression-domain shift rather than predictive accuracy.  Figure 7  and  Table S15  present model projections, not experimentally measured dependencies, together with expression-domain diagnostics. 

Model projections and expression-domain diagnostics in Tiriac pancreatic organoids. Panels ( a – c ) show model-predicted dependency scores, not experimentally measured dependencies. ( a ) Median projected Gene Effect across 38 tumor cases; blue hatched bars highlight  CDK6 ,  CCNE1 , and  MDM4 , whereas gray bars represent the other targets. More negative values indicate stronger projected dependency within each model. ( b ) Tumor-minus-normal projection differences with 95% case-bootstrap confidence intervals; blue points and error bars indicate BH q < 0.05, whereas gray points and error bars indicate BH q ≥ 0.05; the vertical dashed line marks zero difference. ( c ) Tiriac tumor-median projections versus Broad-94 and Sanger-148 median predictions; the dashed line denotes numerical equality, not agreement with measured outcomes. ( d ) Expression-domain diagnostics: cumulative distributions of absolute SMD across 3790 shared features; dotted lines mark |SMD| = 1 and 2. Tiriac lacked measured CRISPR dependencies; these projections require experimental validation. BH, Benjamini–Hochberg; CI, confidence interval; CRISPR, clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats; SMD, standardized mean difference. 

The 20-target median projection ranking in Tiriac tumor cases was concordant with the Broad-94 median prediction ranking (ρ = 0.717; two-sided  p  = 3.71 × 10 −4 ) and the Sanger-148 median prediction ranking (ρ = 0.780;  p  = 4.91 × 10 −5 ;  Figure 7 c). All three vectors were predictions from the same frozen model family, and Tiriac lacked measured CRISPR outcomes. These correlations therefore quantify target-hierarchy consistency rather than independent predictive accuracy. 

Expression-domain diagnostics demonstrated substantial cohort shift. Across the 3790 frozen features shared by the three organoid resources, the median absolute SMD was 0.743 for Tiriac versus Broad-94 and 0.848 for Tiriac versus Sanger-148. The proportions with |SMD| > 1 were 38.7% and 43.4%, respectively, and the proportions with |SMD| > 2 were 13.2% and 14.6% ( Figure 7 d). PCA likewise separated the Tiriac samples from the Broad and Sanger cohorts. We reported these diagnostics without recalibration, feature deletion, target selection, or prediction modification. Accordingly, the Tiriac findings support secondary application and prioritization only, and we did not interpret them as validated dependencies. 

The retrospective coverage-threshold check retained all 20 target models in each external cohort at 80%, 90%, and 95% ( Table S16 ). Coverage was 100% in Broad-94, 97.0–100% in Sanger-148, and 97.33–100% in Tiriac. Because the threshold acts only as an eligibility gate and none of the models failed the stricter requirements, using 90% or 95% would leave the frozen predictions and their reported summaries unchanged. 

Using the unchanged 20-gene predictions, the mean within-organoid Spearman correlation was 0.3428 (95% CI, 0.3026 to 0.3826) in Broad-94 and 0.4294 (95% CI, 0.3926 to 0.4645) in Sanger-148 ( Table S17 ). The corresponding fixed 2D-mean comparator estimates were 0.2057 and 0.1995. Paired expression-minus-fixed-mean differences were +0.1371 (95% CI, 0.0987 to 0.1780) and +0.2299 (95% CI, 0.1981 to 0.2613), respectively. These estimates address gene ordering within each organoid and are not directly comparable to the original macro target-wise correlations of 0.1518 and 0.1015. 

The archived lineage-only predictions yielded mean within-organoid correlations of 0.2745 in Broad and 0.3884 in Sanger. Expression-minus-lineage differences were +0.0684 (95% CI, 0.0334 to 0.1032) and +0.0411 (95% CI, 0.0167 to 0.0656), respectively. Thus, full-panel gene ranking showed a modest retrospective gain over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts ( Table S17 ). This finding does not change the uncertainty in the original Sanger target-wise expression-minus-lineage difference. 

Top-three overlap was 34.04% in Broad and 42.79% in Sanger, compared with 34.75% and 35.36% for lineage-only prediction ( Table S18 ). Paired expression-minus-lineage differences were −0.71 percentage points (95% CI, −4.96 to 3.19) in Broad and +7.43 percentage points (95% CI, 4.28 to 10.74) in Sanger. The Broad interval included zero; superiority for selecting the three strongest measured dependencies was therefore not demonstrated across both cohorts. 

Averaging the 20-gene vectors within each Sanger donor before evaluation gave a mean within-donor correlation of 0.4338 (95% CI, 0.3986 to 0.4675). The paired difference from lineage-only prediction was +0.0402 (95% CI, 0.0157 to 0.0652). Mean top-three overlap was 42.82%, with an expression-minus-lineage difference of +6.94 percentage points (95% CI, 4.17 to 9.95;  Table S19 ). These results were consistent with the organoid-weighted analysis. All within-organoid findings remain retrospective and restricted to the selected 20-gene panel. 

This study tested whether transcriptome-based gene-dependency rankings learned in 2D cancer models retained a detectable but attenuated signal in patient-derived 3D organoids. Transfer was evaluated without outcome-guided retraining. The findings provide limited support for this hypothesis. We selected the expression-only representation in the development cohort. Holdout performance provided internal confirmation conditional on historical target selection. Positive panel-wide rank signals were observed in the Broad-94 exploratory cohort and the Sanger-148 blinded independent external validation cohort. However, macro correlations in Broad, Sanger, and the combined 3D analysis were markedly lower than in the locked 2D holdout. Transfer was therefore incomplete and weak. The findings support modest, context-sensitive preservation of organoid ranking for genes within the preselected panel across independently generated resources. They do not establish cross-platform equivalence. This primary finding is distinct from the secondary within-organoid gene-ranking results in  Section 3.7 . 

Tumor lineage is central to interpreting this transfer. Expression patterns can encode tissue identity and shared transcriptional programs. A model may therefore reproduce differences in dependency between tumor types without identifying the mechanisms responsible for those dependencies. The CRISPR outcomes measure gene-depletion effects, but the fitted expression–dependency relationships remain predictive associations. Their transfer does not establish that the predictive expression features have a causal role in determining dependency. Thus, predictive generalization to organoids and mechanistic generalization are distinct claims. 

For the prespecified target-wise endpoint, the added predictive value of transcriptomic modeling over tumor lineage was not established in the blinded Sanger cohort. The expression-only model exceeded the frozen lineage-only comparator numerically by 0.0462 in macro Spearman correlation. However, the paired donor-cluster bootstrap CI included zero (95% CI, −0.0065 to 0.0986). This result establishes neither superiority nor equivalence. The significant within-lineage permutation result addresses a different question: whether the prediction–outcome association exceeds a null distribution that preserves tumor-type structure. It does not establish superior performance over the lineage-only predictor. Target-specific correlations rank organoids for a given gene. They cannot by themselves establish correct gene ranking within an organoid or an increase in the yield of functional experiments. The study contributes an outcome-blinded assessment of performance attenuation from cell lines to organoids and documents uncertainty in the incremental value of transcriptomic prediction.  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1  remain candidates for prospective investigation. Their multiplicity-adjusted correlations do not establish target-specific superiority over lineage or practical benefit. 

The retrospective within-organoid analysis directly examined this second ranking task. Full 20-gene rankings improved over a fixed 2D-mean ranking and, more modestly, over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts ( Table S17 ). These comparisons are needed because shared differences in average dependency among genes can produce positive within-organoid correlations. A gain in full-panel correlation did not ensure a better short candidate list. Top-three overlap exceeded lineage-only prediction in Sanger but not in Broad ( Table S18 ). The donor-mean sensitivity analysis gave similar Sanger results ( Table S19 ). This is limited retrospective evidence of ranking agreement within a selected panel, not prospective proof that transcriptomic modeling improves experimental yield. It neither replaces the original primary analysis nor resolves its uncertainty about target-wise superiority over lineage. 

Adding copy-number, mutation, and lineage information to gene expression did not reliably improve development performance. DeepDEP and transcriptome-based maps developed for The Cancer Genome Atlas use molecular profiles from cancer cell lines to estimate dependencies in functionally unscreened tumors [ 16 , 17 ]. Synthetic data augmentation and multi-omic integration have also been proposed to address incomplete profiles and capture more complex dependency patterns [ 18 ]. The expression-plus-copy-number candidate yielded the highest development score numerically. However, its advantage over expression-only was negligible, and the paired bootstrap interval included zero. The expression-only representation also showed the greatest feature-set stability and was selected by the prespecified one-standard-error rule. This result does not imply that copy number or mutation is biologically uninformative. This finding applies to the fixed 20-target panel, the available sample size, the 300-feature limit per target, and the linear Elastic Net framework. Within these constraints, the additional data layers did not provide a sufficiently stable incremental signal to justify greater complexity. The broad availability and relatively consistent measurement of transcriptomic data across model collections further strengthens the practical rationale for an expression-only deployment model. 

Performance was lower in the organoid cohorts than in the 2D internal confirmation analysis. The macro Spearman correlation was 0.4094 in the locked 2D holdout, compared with 0.1518 in Broad-94 and 0.1015 in Sanger-148. This attenuation is interpreted as an observed cross-domain effect whose biological and technical causes remain uncertain. Computational alignment studies have documented transcriptional differences between cell lines and tumors [ 20 ]. Next-generation model resources have reported tumor-relevant transcriptional programs and culture-specific dependencies in organoids and spheroids [ 38 ], while patient-derived model collections have shown that culture conditions can modify cellular states in a subset of models [ 40 ]. These studies provide context for plausible explanations, but they do not establish the causes of the performance decline in the present cohorts. Differences in cellular state, growth geometry, medium composition, lineage composition, and functional-screening platforms may contribute individually or jointly. Their contributions were not experimentally disentangled or quantified here. The comparison used different cohorts and assay settings, rather than paired models in which growth conditions and screening platforms were varied under controlled conditions. It therefore cannot attribute the decline to 3D growth itself, identify a dominant mechanism, or exclude contributions from model limitations and cohort composition. Historical target-panel selection also limits interpretation of the 2D reference estimate. The observed attenuation supports evaluating dependency models across data domains without assigning a confirmed biological or technical explanation to the decline. 

The contrasting roles of Broad-94 and Sanger-148 were central to the evidential interpretation. Broad-94 was retained as exploratory because the resource had been accessible during earlier methodological work and donor information was absent from the original evaluation records. The later audit recovered only partial donor information and confirmed a same-donor pair ( Tables S22–S24 ); it did not establish independence of the full cohort. The audited analysis run nevertheless preserved a prediction-before-outcome sequence. Sanger-148, by contrast, was constructed after excluding Broad-linked and same-donor records, and its predictions were cryptographically sealed before the CRISPR outcomes were opened. Donor-clustered uncertainty estimation and donor-aware permutation further preserved the strongest available biological independence structure. Differences in screening libraries, protocols, and computational processing can affect Broad and Sanger dependency estimates [ 10 , 11 ]. Chronos was developed in part to model sources of variation in CRISPR screen dynamics [ 12 ]. Target-wise correlations were positive for 18 of 20 targets in Broad-94 and 17 of 20 in Sanger-148. This pattern suggests that the panel-wide direction was not confined to a single laboratory or processing system. Nevertheless, the Sanger target-wise expression-only-minus-lineage CI included zero, so that cohort did not establish an incremental advantage on the prespecified primary endpoint. Transcriptomic information therefore carried a detectable external rank signal, but part of that signal may reflect lineage-associated structure. 

The target-level findings provided the strongest cross-cohort support for  CCNE1 ,  CDK6 , and  MDM4 .  CCNE1  was positive in both organoid cohorts, passed the FDR threshold in Sanger-148, and yielded one of the largest combined estimates. This is biologically consistent with the recent Sanger organoid dependency map, in which  CCNE1  amplification in esophageal organoids was associated with  CCNE1  dependency [ 39 ].  CDK6  produced the highest target-specific correlation in Sanger-148 and remained in Tier A in the retrospective synthesis. The Broad NextGen study reported enrichment for  CDK6  dependency in glioblastoma models that preserve a tumor-proximal glial transcriptional state. Genetic and pharmacological experiments supported this association in specific genomic contexts [ 38 ]. That work provides an example of how transcriptional state can expose context-dependent  CDK6  vulnerability.  MDM4  also showed a positive ranking signal in both cohorts. However, the current reference set provides less direct organoid-based mechanistic support for  MDM4  than for  CCNE1  or  CDK6 .  MDM4  should therefore be regarded primarily as a hypothesis for targeted functional testing. These findings identify genes whose variation across organoids was predicted with the strongest support. They do not establish that these are the three strongest dependencies within an individual organoid. More generally, their target-specific correlations do not demonstrate drug sensitivity, therapeutic index, or clinical benefit. 

The study also has important limitations. First, historical selection of the fixed 20-gene panel used all 852 2D models. The 171-model holdout was independent of the current candidate representation, feature selection, hyperparameters, and model-fitting decisions. However, it was not fully independent of historical target selection. The holdout therefore provides internal confirmation conditional on that selection, not an unbiased estimate of generalization for the complete analysis pipeline. Selection-induced optimism cannot be excluded or quantified by the reported fixed-panel analysis. Second, all external performance estimates are restricted to the preselected 20 targets. Sample independence in Sanger does not establish dependency transferability for unselected genes or across the genome. Target-panel bootstrap intervals quantify sensitivity to panel composition rather than sampling uncertainty for new patients or organoids. Third, donor metadata could be recovered for only 25 of the 94 Broad models, and one same-donor pair was confirmed. The remaining 69 models and cross-source donor equivalence could not be resolved. The partial-cluster and donor-documented-subset sensitivities do not establish full-cohort independence. Original Broad CIs, permutation results and the uncertainty weights used in retrospective synthesis remain conditional on model-level independence and may overstate precision if additional dependence is present. Consequently, Broad and Sanger uncertainty estimates and effective sample sizes are not directly comparable. Fourth, Sanger-148 was dominated by colorectal and esophageal organoids. Ovarian, pancreatic, and gastric subgroups were too small to establish lineage-specific generalizability. Fifth, the differing Broad Chronos Gene Effect and Sanger-corrected LFC scales precluded direct absolute-error and calibration comparisons [ 10 , 11 , 12 ]. Sixth, target-specific linear expression-only models cannot fully represent genotype-transcriptome interactions, network structure, cell–cell interactions, or the incompletely modeled stromal and immune compartments of organoid systems [ 24 , 25 , 29 ]. Finally, the Tiriac normal group contained only 11 cases and lacked measured CRISPR dependencies, matched drug-response outcomes, and clinical endpoints. The study consequently establishes rank-transfer evidence, not prospective therapeutic utility. 

Adjustment for broad tumor-type labels and comparison with a lineage-only reference cannot fully separate lineage-associated transcriptional structure from other predictive signals. The present analyses therefore do not establish the causal basis of the transferred associations. Lineage adjustment and descriptive expression-domain diagnostics do not experimentally separate the causes of performance attenuation. The present design cannot estimate the individual contributions of culture conditions, cellular state, cohort composition, and screening technology to the observed cross-domain effect. 

Future work should test the Tier A targets using direct CRISPR perturbation in preregistered, tumor-type-balanced organoid cohorts. Gene-depletion effects should be related to target-specific inhibitor responses and, where available, to matched clinical information. Distinguishing biological from technical contributions to attenuation would require larger cohorts with patient-level identifiers, paired 2D and 3D models, controlled variation in culture conditions, and harmonized or cross-platform CRISPR measurements. Nonlinear models incorporating network or pathway structure, domain-adaptation strategies, and richer multi-omic representations are also reasonable candidates for evaluation. Their value, however, should be assessed without outcome-guided retuning and under the same prediction-lock and independent-validation discipline used here. The results document modest rank transfer within the selected gene panel. Whether the retrospective within-organoid ranking gains improve prospective experimental prioritization beyond tumor lineage remains to be established. 

This study shows that transcriptome-based models learned in 2D cancer models retain a weak ability to rank patient-derived 3D organoids by dependency for genes in the preselected panel, without retraining and with substantial attenuation. More complex omic representations did not provide a reliable advantage over the expression-only model. Its macro Spearman correlation of 0.4094 in the locked 2D holdout provides internal confirmation conditional on historical target selection, not an unbiased generalization estimate. Performance decreased to 0.1518 in the Broad-94 exploratory cohort and to 0.1015 in the Sanger-148 blinded, independent external validation cohort. The Sanger association nevertheless exceeded the permutation-based null expectation. Eighteen of 20 target estimates were positive in Broad-94, 17 of 20 were positive in Sanger-148, and 15 of 20 were positive in both cohorts. The combined 3D macro correlation of 0.1231 therefore supports a modest, directionally reproducible rank-transfer signal rather than complete cross-system generalizability. The primary endpoint did not establish added predictive value beyond tumor lineage in Sanger. These results support transfer of predictive associations without establishing their causal basis. Broad uncertainty and retrospective pooled evidence remain conditional on incomplete donor ascertainment. The attenuation is an observed cross-domain effect; its biological and technical causes were not experimentally disentangled and remain uncertain. 

CCNE1 ,  CDK6 , and  MDM4  received the strongest cross-cohort support and represent priorities for direct functional investigation rather than established therapeutic sensitivities. The Tiriac pancreatic projection showed that the frozen models could be applied to an independent transcriptomic cohort while retaining aspects of the target hierarchy. However, measured CRISPR outcomes were unavailable, and expression-domain shift was substantial. These limitations preclude interpreting that analysis as validation. The separate retrospective within-organoid analysis showed modest gains over lineage-only prediction for full 20-gene rankings in both cohorts, but a top-three overlap gain only in Sanger. These findings provide limited evidence for ranking genes within the selected panel. They do not establish dependency transferability for unselected genes or at genome scale. Prospective experimental prioritization beyond tumor lineage remains unestablished. The computational approach does not replace direct organoid CRISPR screening, pharmacological validation, or assessment of clinical utility. Prospective evaluation in larger, tumor-type-balanced, preregistered organoid cohorts will be required to establish the biological robustness and translational value of the prioritized targets.

SECTION 2.7. Sanger-148 Cohort Construction and Blinded Independent External Validation 
 2.7. Sanger-148 Cohort Construction and Blinded Independent External Validation The independent Sanger 3D validation cohort was constructed from the RNA-seq and CRISPR-screening records of the tumor-derived organoid dependency map [ 39 ]. Before any Sanger target outcome was accessed, 162 organoids from 157 unique donors with both RNA-seq profiles and quality-controlled CRISPR-screening records were screened for eligibility. To prevent reuse of samples represented in the Broad exploratory layer or elsewhere in the Broad NextGen resource, Sanger sample and donor identifiers were cross-referenced against the Broad mapping records and the Broad-94 registry [ 38 , 39 ]. Thirteen Broad-linked organoids and one additional organoid from the same donor as a Broad-linked model were conservatively excluded. The final cohort therefore comprised 148 organoids from 144 unique donors, with no exact Broad-94 identity or identifiable same-donor Broad overlap. Cohort construction, harmonization, and the outcome-blinded lock procedure are summarized in  Table S6 . The final cohort included five tumor types: 81 colorectal organoids from 79 donors, 49 esophageal organoids from 49 donors, 11 ovarian organoids from 9 donors, 4 pancreatic organoids from 4 donors, and 3 gastric organoids from 3 donors. RNA-seq input and experimental CRISPR coverage for all 20 locked targets were available for every included organoid. Colorectal and esophageal subgroup analyses were prespecified as secondary, the ovarian subgroup was exploratory, and the pancreatic and gastric subgroups were treated as descriptive because of their small sample sizes. The primary external-validation analysis used all 148 organoids, with donor structure preserved in uncertainty estimation and sensitivity analyses. Sanger TPM values were transformed as log2(TPM + 1) and aligned to the frozen expression-only feature space using Entrez Gene identifiers. For each target, the 300 expression features stored in the corresponding 2D deployment-model bundle were used in their locked order. Across targets, 3790 of 3845 unique locked genes were measured in the Sanger matrix; 55 genes were absent. Target-specific coverage ranged from 291 to 300 of 300 features (97.0–100%), exceeding the prespecified 80% minimum for all targets. Missing features were neither re-estimated from the external cohort nor replaced using Sanger-derived values; only the imputation and standardization objects learned in 2D data and the fixed Elastic Net coefficients were applied. A pre-outcome audit identified that the original coverage-key array had incorrectly counted the 55 entirely absent genes as present. The coverage metadata were corrected before outcome access, while the original and corrected blinded predictions remained bitwise identical. Before the Sanger CRISPR outcomes were opened, the 148-organoid × 20-target blinded prediction matrix, cohort registry, protocol, model manifest, and feature manifest were written and sealed with SHA-256 digests; all 11 prespecified seal checks passed. Only after this lock was established were the Sanger outcomes accessed once and merged with the predictions in the frozen sample and target order, yielding 2960 finite prediction-outcome pairs. The external outcome was the corrected and scaled gene-level CRISPR LFC, with more negative values indicating stronger dependency. For the 13 organoids screened with two CRISPR libraries, the official Sanger covariate was applied: minLib was selected when the code was 1 and v1_1 when the code was 0. The available quality-controlled screen was used for single-library organoids. This externally defined selection rule was fixed before inspection of the target outcomes. The primary external-validation estimand was the target-specific Spearman correlation between the blinded predictions and Sanger LFC values, followed by the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). This primary estimand evaluates the ordering of organoids for a fixed gene and does not assess the ordering of genes within an organoid. Rank correlation was selected because the Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values are on assay-specific scales and were not assumed to be numerically comparable. Uncertainty was estimated with 5000 donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, retaining all organoids from a sampled donor together; 95% CIs were obtained from the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. For the null distribution, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, predictions were held fixed, and each donor-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was permuted within tumor-type/lineage strata for 5000 iterations. The two-sided empirical  p -value was calculated as (1 + the number of absolute null macro correlations at least as large as the observed absolute macro correlation)/5001. Target-specific permutation  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. A lineage-only comparator, frozen before access to Sanger outcomes and based on patient-weighted 2D lineage means, was applied to the same cohort. We evaluated the expression-only-minus-lineage macro difference using paired donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, and we also calculated partial Spearman correlations adjusted for the primary tumor-type indicators. The influence of repeated donors was examined in two additional analyses: one retained the lexicographically first sample identifier per donor, and the other averaged predictions and outcomes within donor. An independent replay recomputed cohort size, donor count, overlap exclusions, feature coverage, target and prediction order, the complete 2960-pair grid, correlation statistics, bootstrap and permutation results, lineage adjustment, and file hashes; all 22 checks passed. As detailed in  Table S6 , no model, target, feature, hyperparameter, or prespecified primary metric was changed after outcome unlock. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints were evaluated separately using the unchanged predictions.
SECTION 2.8. Retrospective Cross-Cohort Transportability Synthesis 
 2.8. Retrospective Cross-Cohort Transportability Synthesis The target-specific results from the Broad-94 exploratory evaluation and the Sanger-148 blinded independent external validation were combined retrospectively only after the two cohort-specific analyses had been completed [ 38 , 39 ]. The fixed 20-target panel defined in  Section 2.2  was aligned using Entrez Gene identifiers, and no targets were removed based on effect direction, magnitude, or statistical significance. Because Broad used Chronos Gene Effect values and Sanger used corrected CRISPR LFC values, the synthesis addressed target-specific rank transportability rather than absolute-scale equivalence. The Tiriac pancreatic projection was excluded from this synthesis because it did not contain measured genome-scale CRISPR outcomes. The synthesis protocol is summarized in  Table S7 . Directional concordance was defined as a positive Spearman correlation for the same target in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148. The number of common-positive targets was compared with a one-sided exact binomial distribution with  n  = 20 and a null success probability of 0.50. This comparison was interpreted only as a descriptive sign benchmark: dependence among targets limits the binomial interpretation, and the synthesis was defined after the cohort-specific results were known. Agreement in relative target performance was evaluated separately using the two-sided Spearman correlation between the vectors of 20 Broad and 20 Sanger target-specific correlations. For target-level pooling, each cohort-specific Spearman correlation was transformed to the Fisher-z scale. We derived inverse-variance weights from the cohort-specific uncertainty intervals obtained from the lineage-stratified Broad bootstrap and the donor-clustered Sanger bootstrap. Because Broad donor structure was incompletely ascertained, its original uncertainty weights may overstate precision; the synthesis is conditional on this unresolved model-level independence assumption. A fixed-effects pooled estimate was calculated for each target and then transformed back to the Spearman correlation scale. The resulting two-sided meta-analytic  p -values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. Because only two 3D cohorts were available, random-effects estimates and the I 2  heterogeneity statistic were retained as descriptive sensitivity measures and were not used as confirmatory decision criteria. The panel-wide combined 3D estimate was defined as the arithmetic mean of the 20 fixed-effect target correlations. Sensitivity to target-panel composition was assessed by resampling the 20 locked targets with replacement for 20,000 iterations, then recalculating the combined macro correlation at each iteration. We reported the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles as a descriptive 95% interval; this interval characterizes sensitivity to the composition of the fixed target panel and is not a new-patient or new-organoid sampling interval. Attenuation from 2D to 3D was calculated for each target by subtracting the corresponding locked 2D holdout correlation from the Broad or Sanger correlation, with panel-level summaries obtained using the same target-bootstrap structure. To evaluate whether cross-target dependence could explain the common-positive count, we performed a post hoc, correlation-aware simultaneous permutation analysis. Predictions remained fixed. In Broad, each ModelID-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was retained as a block; in Sanger, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, and each donor-level 20-target outcome vector was retained as a block. These vectors were permuted simultaneously among units within each cohort for 50,000 iterations, preserving the cross-target covariance structure. We recalculated the number of targets remaining positive in both cohorts at each iteration and calculated the one-sided empirical  p -value with the plus-one correction. This analysis was explicitly treated as a post hoc sensitivity analysis and did not constitute a third external validation. Within each cohort, we subtracted the target-specific lineage-only correlation from the corresponding expression-only correlation. We used a one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the 20 paired differences to assess the incremental contribution of expression beyond lineage. A retrospective evidence classification was used for reporting only. Tier A required a positive correlation in both cohorts, a meta-analytic FDR q < 0.05, and target-level FDR q < 0.05 in at least one individual cohort. Tier B indicated positive correlations in both cohorts without fulfillment of all Tier A criteria. Tier C indicated a non-positive correlation in at least one cohort. As specified in  Table S7 , the evidence tiers did not alter the locked panel or any model component, and no retraining, target or feature reselection, hyperparameter tuning, metric switching, or direction-based target removal was permitted during the synthesis.
SECTION 2.11. Code and Reproducibility Resources 
 2.11. Code and Reproducibility Resources The study’s reproducibility package is available on Zenodo ( https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22472622 ). The package includes custom analysis and figure-generation scripts; frozen cohort, split, target, and feature registries; trained model artifacts; locked predictions; analysis-ready inputs and processed outputs; bootstrap and permutation results; figure source data; software-environment records; and SHA-256 integrity manifests. It also contains the code, inputs, and results for the primary analyses, retrospective within-organoid gene ranking, Tiriac missing-feature imputation sensitivity, and Broad cohort-size and donor sensitivity analyses. Complete original third-party data releases remain available from the repositories identified in the Data Availability Statement.
SECTION 3.4. Blinded Independent External Validation in Sanger-148 Organoids 
 3.4. Blinded Independent External Validation in Sanger-148 Organoids The outcome-blinded Sanger eligibility pool comprised 162 organoids from 157 donors with both RNA-seq profiles and quality-controlled CRISPR screening records. After excluding 13 Broad-linked organoids and one additional organoid from the same donor as a Broad-linked model, the independent validation cohort contained 148 organoids from 144 unique donors. Of the 3845 unique locked expression features used across the 20 target models, 3790 were measured in the Sanger matrix; target-specific coverage ranged from 291 to 300 of 300 features (97.0–100%). The 148 × 20 blind prediction matrix and the associated manifests were SHA-256 sealed before CRISPR LFC outcomes were accessed, and all 11 prespecified pre-outcome seal checks passed. Panel-wide validation, target-level results, and robustness analyses are summarized in  Figure 5  and  Table S13 . Figure 5 Blinded independent external validation in Sanger-148 organoids. ( a ) Cohort construction and tumor-type composition. Fourteen organoids were excluded from 162 eligible organoids representing 157 donors, leaving 148 organoids from 144 donors. ( b ) Panel-wide expression-only, lineage-only, and paired-difference estimates; error bars show 95% donor-cluster bootstrap confidence intervals. ( c ) Target-specific Spearman correlations with 95% confidence intervals. Blue points denote Benjamini–Hochberg q < 0.05; the dotted line denotes the macro correlation. ( d ) Robustness and tumor-type subgroup estimates. Predictions were SHA-256-sealed before outcome access; the primary macro correlation was 0.1015, 17/20 target estimates were positive, and the two-sided within-tumor-type donor-level permutation  p -value was 0.00040. In ( a ), arrows indicate cohort selection. In ( b ), blue, gray, and orange points and error bars denote expression-only, lineage-only, and expression-minus-lineage estimates, respectively. In ( c ), gray points denote q ≥ 0.05, and error bars use the corresponding point colors. In ( d ), filled circles denote cohort-level estimates, with the primary estimate in blue and the sensitivity and lineage-only reference estimates in gray; open squares denote tumor-type subgroup estimates. Black dashed vertical lines in ( b – d ) mark zero. BH, Benjamini–Hochberg; CI, confidence interval; SHA-256, 256-bit Secure Hash Algorithm. As shown in  Figure 5 a,b, the frozen expression-only model achieved a macro target-wise Spearman correlation of 0.1015 in the independent Sanger-148 cohort (95% CI, 0.0637–0.1357). Seventeen of the 20 target estimates were positive. After repeated organoids were averaged within donors, 5000 two-sided permutations of complete 20-target outcome vectors within tumor-type strata yielded  p  = 0.00040. Thus, the locked 2D model carried a detectable rank-ordering signal into the independent 3D cohort, although the effect size was modest. As shown in  Figure 5 c and  Table S13 , the largest target-specific correlations were observed for  CDK6  (ρ = 0.3221; 95% CI, 0.1666–0.4702),  MDM4  (ρ = 0.2992; 95% CI, 0.1483–0.4410),  KEAP1  (ρ = 0.2967; 95% CI, 0.1256–0.4464),  CTNNB1  (ρ = 0.2831; 95% CI, 0.1031–0.4563), and  CCNE1  (ρ = 0.2792; 95% CI, 0.1014–0.4493). After Benjamini–Hochberg correction of the 20 target-level permutation  p -values, only  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1  met q < 0.05 (q = 0.0040 for each).  CCND3 ,  EGFR , and  NFE2L2  were the three targets with negative correlations; no targets were removed based on direction or significance. The tumor-type-adjusted macro Spearman correlation was 0.0882, whereas the frozen lineage-only reference achieved 0.0553 (95% CI, 0.0168–0.0917). The expression-only-minus-lineage difference was +0.0462, but its paired donor-cluster bootstrap interval included zero (95% CI, −0.0065 to 0.0986). The primary target-wise endpoint therefore did not establish superiority beyond tumor-origin information. The one-model-per-donor estimate (ρ = 0.1014) and donor-mean estimate (ρ = 0.0990) closely matched the primary result. Tumor-type subgroup estimates were 0.1030 for colorectal, 0.0654 for esophageal, 0.1755 for ovarian, 0.0300 for pancreatic, and 0.1500 for gastric organoids ( Figure 5 d). Ovarian results were exploratory, whereas pancreatic and gastric estimates were descriptive because of their small sample sizes. An independent replay reproduced the cohort composition, target and prediction order, feature coverage, primary and sensitivity statistics, lock records, and file hashes, with all 22 predefined checks passing. No model retraining, target or feature selection, hyperparameter adjustment, or change to the prespecified primary metric occurred after the Sanger outcomes were opened. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints are reported separately in  Section 3.7 . These findings support a statistically detectable but quantitatively modest independent 3D rank-transfer signal, with the strongest target-level support for  CDK6 ,  MDM4 , and  CCNE1 .
SECTION Data Availability Statement 
 Data Availability Statement This study analyzed publicly available or openly released datasets. The 2D molecular profiles and CRISPR Gene Effect data were obtained from the DepMap Public 26Q1 release and the CCLE [ 3 , 4 , 13 , 14 ] ( https://depmap.org/portal/data_page/?tab=allData ) (accessed on 26 September 2026). The Broad NextGen organoid data were obtained from the  NextGen Model Manuscript 2026  resource and the associated Figshare dataset [ 38 ] ( https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.29472362.v1 ). The Sanger tumor-derived organoid dependency data were obtained from the associated Figshare dataset [ 39 ] ( https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.28339340 ). The Tiriac pancreatic organoid RNA-seq data were obtained from the GDC ORGANOID-PANCREATIC project [ 32 ] ( https://portal.gdc.cancer.gov/projects/ORGANOID-PANCREATIC ) (accessed on 26 September 2026). The analysis code, trained models, processed data, and supporting results are available in the study’s reproducibility repository on Zenodo [ 41 ]. Original source datasets remain available from their respective repositories under the applicable access and licensing conditions.

README_RELEVANT
14 ### minipool_guide_metadata.csv
15 This table provides the barcode sequence (“Construct_Barcode”) and target gene (“Gene”) for each of the 2000 sgRNAs included in the minipool library. See Fig. 5c and Extended Data Fig. 9a for the schematic and graphical representations of this library, respectively. This library targets 1,000 genes, including 250 test genes, 650 negative control genes, and 100 positive control (i.e., common essential) genes.
18 ### minipool_library_composition.csv
19 This table shows the membership of each of the nine core functional classes (“Integrin”, “ECM Adhesion”, “Actin Regulation”, “Adherens Junction”, “Tight Junction”, “Lipid Metabolism”, ”Wnt Signaling”, “PI3K mTOR Signaling”, and “Cell Cycle”) targeted by the minipool library. The majority of the test genes of this library belong to one or more of these core functional classes, while the rest of them (“Other”) do not belong to any of these classes but are still included in the library based on specific interests. See Extended Data Fig. 9a for the graphical representations of this library.
22 ### minipool_screen_metadata.csv
23 This table provides the metadata, including model ID (“DepMap ID”), growth format, and type of culture medium (“Serum status” and “Culture medium”), as well as the QC results for each of the minipool screens (n = 18). These QC results include log2-fold change correlation between the replicates (“replicate LFC correlation”), total sequencing reads (“Total reads”), and two metrics based on the gene effect distributions of negative and positive controls (“NNMD” and “ROC-AUC”).
26 ### minipool_sequence_metadata.csv
27 This table provides the metadata, including model ID (“DepMap ID”), growth format, and type of culture medium (“Serum status” and “Culture medium”), as well as the QC results for each of the minipool screen sequencing attempts (n = 34). Note that 16 out of the 18 minipool screens were conducted with technical duplicates. One of the replicates (0178_Adh_Nat_Serum_Dome_RepB) was removed from the further analysis due to the low NNMD score. The read count of the PCR products of genomic DNA prepared from Cas12a-null 293T cells that were infected with the minipool library (HEK_CasNull_MP_control) is also displayed.
30 ### model_metadata.csv
86 ### minipool_chronos_gene_effects.csv
87 Chronos gene dependency scores were determined based on the results of the minipool screens (screen n = 18) for each of the 1,000 genes evaluated in these screens (100 positive control genes, 650 negative control genes, and 250 test genes). These Chronos scores provided the basis for the minipool screen analyses whose results are presented in Figs. 5d-g, Extended Data Figs. 9c-g.
90 ### minipool_growth_format_culture_medium_per_class.csv
91 After calculating the effects of growth formats and culture media on gene dependencies as coefficients of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; the coefficient values are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”), two analyses were conducted to evaluate the gene class-specific effects of these two components (i.e., growth format and culture medium) of culture conditions: 1) Mann-Whitney U test to compare the distributions of coefficients between in-class and out-of-class test genes for each of the nine core functional classes of minipool targets (results shown in Extended Data Figs. 9f,g); 2) F-test to compare the variances of the coefficients between in-class and out-of-class test genes for each of the nine core functional classes of minipool targets (results shown in Fig. 5g). This table summarizes the results of these two analyses, where columns starting with “GF” indicate the effects of growth formats and those starting with “CM” represent the effects of culture media. The scores shown here underlie the plot shown in Fig. 5g and Extended Data Figs. 9f,g.
94 ### minipool_growth_format_tests.csv
95 For each of the test genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 250), the effect of the growth formats (Matrigel dome culture [dome] vs monolayer culture on plastic plates [plastic]) on gene dependency was extracted as a coefficient of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; these coefficients are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”) and the significance of the effect was scored by the two-sided linear regression t-test (“pvalue”). FDR (adjusted P-value) was calculated using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure. The mean Chronos scores in the screens on dome cultures (n = 11) and plastic cultures (n = 7) are also shown (“true_dome_mean” and “true_plastic_mean”, respectively). The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Fig. 5d and Extended Data Fig. 9f.
98 ### minipool_regression_coefficients.csv
99 For each of the genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 1000), the effects of the growth formats (Matrigel dome culture [dome] vs monolayer culture on plastic plates [plastic) and the culture media (serum-free OPAC medium vs serum-containing RPMI/FBS medium) on gene dependency were extracted as coefficients of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods). This spreadsheet shows the coefficients representing the effect of growth formats (“C(Growth_Format)[T.Plastic]”) and the effect of culture media (“C(Serum_status)[T.Serum-free]”) for each gene. The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Figs 5d-g and Extended Data Figs. 9f,g.
102 ### minipool_regression_pvalues.csv
106 ### minipool_serum_status_tests.csv
107 For each of the test genes assessed by the minipool screen (gene n = 250), the effect of the culture media (serum-free OPAC medium vs serum-containing RPMI/FBS medium) on gene dependency was extracted as a coefficient of the linear regression model (see “Minipool screen: analysis” subsection of Methods; these coefficients are shown in “minipool_regression_coefficients. csv”) and the significance of the effect was scored by the two-sided linear regression t-test (“pvalue”). FDR (adjusted P-value) was calculated using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure. The mean Chronos scores in the screens with serum-containing RPMI/FBS (n = 9) and serum-free OPAC (n = 9) are also shown (“true_serum_mean” and “true_serum_free_mean”, respectively). The scores shown here underlie the plots shown in Fig. 5e and Extended Data Fig. 9g.
130 ### organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv
134 ### organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tests_full.csv
135 Gene dependencies were compared between the NextGen models that were grown as 3D organoids (n = 94) vs lineage-matched 2D adherent models (n = 433) (gene n = 18159). Mean dependency difference (“MeanDifference”), the statistical significance of the difference assessed by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test (“MannWhitneyPValue”), as well as the adjusted P-values calculated by the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure (“MannWhitneyFDR”) were scored. Common essential genes, which were removed from the analysis of differential dependency genes (see “organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv”), were also labeled (“DropEssential”). This spreadsheet was used to compile the list of 200 differential dependency genes between 2D and 3D models (see “organoids_vs_2d_gene_dependency_tail_enrichment_tests_full.csv”).

**Verdict: FAIL. G1 is not ready to pass, including with narrowing.** The package establishes usable environmental contrasts, but it has not established an identifiable transfer endpoint, independent validation cohort, or adequate preprocessing provenance. These are substantive gaps.

I read the requested records and handoff §§5–7. All 12 manifested local files match their recorded sizes and SHA-256 hashes. Broad CSV checks reproduce the matrix dimensions, pairing counts, and reported direction correlations. I made no edits and inspected no additional Sanger outcome values. Current Figshare manifests and the S3 Zenodo archive were inaccessible through the available routes, so their contents remain independently unverified.

1. **[CRITICAL] The named question is not yet an operational, answerable estimand.**  
   [g1_question_candidate.md:4](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/reviews/g1_question_candidate.md:4) leaves “transfer degradation of dependency behavior” undefined. A shift in dependency levels, loss of expression–dependency association, and loss of predictive rank correlation are different outcomes. The required genome-wide development matrices are still unavailable.

   **Fix:** Name one numerical transfer endpoint, its reference population, comparator, eligible targets, and required inputs. Demonstrate that those inputs can actually be joined. Published NextGen-versus-traditional test tables cannot replace individual-level prediction data while retaining the same question; that fallback is a different descriptive study.

2. **[CRITICAL] Outcome-guided panel selection creates a circularity risk that acknowledgment does not resolve.**  
   [provenance_v1.md:7](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/provenance_v1.md:7) acknowledges selection using Broad 2D–3D dependency differences. The proposed transfer endpoint may reuse those differences or their constituent observations. Conditioning on lineage and baseline strength does not automatically remove this selection effect.

   **Fix:** Trace the exact selection inputs and their overlap with every proposed evaluation cohort. Treat Broad reuse as development evidence. External confirmation can support a claim conditional on the fixed selected panel only after excluding donor overlap and outcome-dependent shared processing. Do not generalize to unselected genes.

3. **[CRITICAL] Preprocessing independence remains unaudited.**  
   [provenance_v1.md:27](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/provenance_v1.md:27) records a flag rather than a resolved dependency graph. Choosing a study-specific release does not establish independence. Sanger’s public pipeline also contains transformations fitted using outcomes across samples, including spline correction and ComBat. This establishes an audit requirement, not proof that A and C are contaminated. [Sanger processing code](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Garnett-Lab/SangerOrganoidBiobank/main/scripts/CRISPR_pipeline.R).

   **Fix:** Record which observations fitted each correction, library parameter, normalization, and selection rule. Establish whether any evaluation outcomes influenced development artifacts. Where they did, reconstruct development-only processing or obtain independent evidence. Reserve “transductive covariate preprocessing” for covariate-only operations.

4. **[HIGH] Seven models is the overall count, not the independent count for either contrast.**  
   The pair keys in [identifiability_audit.json:14](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/identifiability_audit.json:14) imply:

   | Contrast | Matched contrasts | Distinct models | Verified independent donors |
   |---|---:|---:|---|
   | Medium | 5 | **4** | Unresolved |
   | Format | 7 | **6** | Unresolved |

   ACH-003040 contributes twice to each contrast type and is the only complete four-condition model. Treating all contrasts equally also gives it extra weight.

   **Fix:** Report contrast-specific model and donor counts; define weights and retain shared-model covariance. Genes are correlated targets, not additional patients. A joint bootstrap preserves dependence only if its grouping and estimand are correct; it does not create additional biological support.

5. **[HIGH] “Heterogeneity dominates” is unsupported by the reported sign-flip statistic.**  
   [identifiability_audit.json:53](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/identifiability_audit.json:53) turns any positive and negative observed changes into evidence of biological heterogeneity. My Broad-only check found:

   | Gene group | Medium: both signs | Format: both signs |
   |---|---:|---:|
   | 250 test genes | 90.8% | 98.4% |
   | 750 controls | **92.1%** | **98.1%** |

   For illustration, independent symmetric zero-effect noise produces both signs with probability \(1-2^{1-k}\): 93.75% for five observations and 98.44% for seven. That is not a calibrated null for this dependent design, but it demonstrates why sign flips alone prove little.

   **Fix:** Retract “heterogeneity dominates” and “average effects are near-uninterpretable.” Assess magnitudes against technical variation and controls. Report meaningful opposing effects where supported. Do not expand into poorly supported context strata because tiny estimates cross zero.

6. **[HIGH] The required observation-level provenance and attrition registry is missing.**  
   [provenance_v1.md:26](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/provenance_v1.md:26) leaves donor crosswalks open. The record does not supply the handoff’s complete model/donor/origin/laboratory/library/condition/replicate table or attempted-condition attrition.

   The Broad README documents 34 sequencing attempts and a removed replicate; the analysis matrix contains collapsed screen estimates. Zero matrix missingness does not establish complete experimental coverage.

   **Fix:** Build the observation table and distinguish unattempted conditions, growth failures, QC exclusions, and unavailable metadata. Recover replicate identities and document which uncertainty components are estimable. Resolve ACH-003052 or exclude it from claims requiring the missing annotation.

7. **[HIGH] Treatment scope and confounding need explicit limits.**  
   The interventions change bundles: plastic versus Matrigel dome changes matrix exposure as well as geometry; OPAC versus RPMI/FBS changes medium composition beyond serum. Sparse condition coverage also limits separation of model, lineage, origin, and interaction effects.

   **Fix:** Name the actual treatment bundles. Restrict intervention interpretations to observed within-model comparisons. Do not claim isolated geometry, serum, genotype modification, or causal explanation of external transfer failure. Across-cohort association remains compatible with laboratory, library, state, and population differences.

8. **[HIGH] The Sanger validation outcome is not established by the downloaded candidate file.**  
   [provenance_v1.md:33](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/provenance_v1.md:33) reports 930 ACH columns, whereas the covariates describe HCM-SANG organoids. A crosswalk cannot substitute for establishing whether the required organoid measurements are present. The continuous LFC matrix is named but absent from the supplied manifest.

   **Fix:** Establish object identities, cohort membership, screen/library identifiers, target coverage, and the authoritative continuous-outcome route through schema and documentation. Resolve duplicate-library selection. Binary BAGEL calls require a different endpoint and cannot silently replace continuous rank-transfer evidence. Ranking avoids absolute-scale equivalence; it does not eliminate assay effects.

9. **[HIGH] The Sanger glimpse is disclosed, but its disposition is insufficient.**  
   [exposure_log.jsonl:2](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/exposure_log.jsonl:2) labels 18 cells “de minimis” without identifying their targets, samples, or relationship to eligible C observations. The 930-column schema makes even the description “Sanger glimpse” uncertain.

   **Fix:** Recover the displayed identifiers from retained transcripts without reopening values; map them to candidate cohorts and document downstream decisions. At minimum, affected claims lose an entirely outcome-unseen designation. Conservatively exclude exposed donors or targets where appropriate, or use fresh evidence if influence cannot be bounded. **The entire Sanger resource need not automatically be discarded, but current handling does not justify an intact blinding claim.**

10. **[HIGH] Layer B cannot become untouched merely through a later split.**  
    [partitions_provisional.json:8](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/partitions_provisional.json:8) proposes carving B from Layer A after whole-panel outcome inspection and aggregate analyses.

    **Fix:** Reserve genuinely uninspected evidence or explicitly classify later evaluation on these observations as reused-data engineering evaluation. Gene or pathway partitions within the same measured models do not establish independent biological tasks. Final manifests must record exposure history alongside membership.

11. **[HIGH] A plausible novelty gap remains, but novelty has not been established at the proposed resolution.**  
    [novelty_audit_v1.md:13](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/reviews/novelty_audit_v1.md:13) overstates “nobody has tested.” S1 reports individual-gene environmental findings and paired results, not only class summaries. S2 includes context-specific functional findings; S3 already examines transfer attenuation and distinguishes target-wise prediction from within-organoid ranking. [S1](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10843-7), [S2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13581617/), [S3](https://www.mdpi.com/2079-7737/15/19/1782).

    **Fix:** Complete the listed supplement/code reads and preserve exact searches and exclusions locally. Audit each precise claim against prior figures, targets, and endpoints. The potentially open contribution is a bounded relationship between measured environmental contrasts and independently evaluated transfer degradation—not heterogeneity alone or familiar lipid/adhesion effects. Failure of S3 to answer that question does not establish that this design can answer it.

12. **[HIGH] Falsification and precision rules remain inadequate.**  
    [g1_question_candidate.md:7](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/reviews/g1_question_candidate.md:7) offers several movable alternatives. “No association” is not a falsifier when intervals are wide. A positive association can coexist with an effect below δ. Choosing δ from attainable precision risks redefining scientific importance to fit available data.

    **Fix:** Define one primary parameter, a scientifically justified practical threshold, disjoint decision rules, and an inconclusive region. Perform a precision-feasibility assessment using four/six environmental model clusters and the actual eligible transfer donors. Seven models do not automatically prohibit every panel-wide association; they do not justify an adaptive gene-class × genotype × context search either.

13. **[MEDIUM] Manifest completeness and reproducibility fall short of “verified provenance.”**  
    [source_manifest_sanger_figshare.json:1](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/source_manifest_sanger_figshare.json:1) omits the downloaded Jaccard and VAF files. Neither manifest pins an article version or preserves the authoritative manifest response. Local hash agreement establishes file integrity, not source accuracy. Also, [audit_minipool.py:59](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/src/audit_minipool.py:59) does not regenerate the JSON’s direction-check results.

    **Fix:** Manifest every imported source, pin versions/commits, retain source responses and license evidence, and make the audit regenerate its reported outputs. Document the primary-source QC discrepancy: Methods state 500,000 reads, while Extended Data Fig. 9 states five million.

The required narrowing is substantial: **one fixed environmental contrast, one fixed eligible target family, and one panel-wide associational transfer parameter**, conditional on the selected genes and measured models. For example, medium contrasts in dome have four model supports. Remove adaptive context-stratification and causal-attribution promises unless additional evidence supplies identification. Published group-comparison tables could support a separate descriptive concordance question, which needs its own novelty justification.

Before G1 can pass, complete the crosswalk/count/attrition table; resolve preprocessing independence and the actual transfer-data route; correct the heterogeneity interpretation; adjudicate exposure and B reuse; and document an exact, plausibly novel, feasible question. Phase 2 can then finalize the endpoint, meaningful margins, baselines, uncertainty procedure, multiplicity, eligibility, partitions, invalid-result rules, and hashes required for Lock 1. Those later implementation requirements should not be confused with the unresolved G1 evidence requirements.

At minimum, affected claims lose an entirely outcome-unseen designation. Conservatively exclude exposed donors or targets where appropriate, or use fresh evidence if influence cannot be bounded. **The entire Sanger resource need not automatically be discarded, but current handling does not justify an intact blinding claim.**

10. **[HIGH] Layer B cannot become untouched merely through a later split.**  
    [partitions_provisional.json:8](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/partitions_provisional.json:8) proposes carving B from Layer A after whole-panel outcome inspection and aggregate analyses.

    **Fix:** Reserve genuinely uninspected evidence or explicitly classify later evaluation on these observations as reused-data engineering evaluation. Gene or pathway partitions within the same measured models do not establish independent biological tasks. Final manifests must record exposure history alongside membership.

11. **[HIGH] A plausible novelty gap remains, but novelty has not been established at the proposed resolution.**  
    [novelty_audit_v1.md:13](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/reviews/novelty_audit_v1.md:13) overstates “nobody has tested.” S1 reports individual-gene environmental findings and paired results, not only class summaries. S2 includes context-specific functional findings; S3 already examines transfer attenuation and distinguishes target-wise prediction from within-organoid ranking. [S1](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10843-7), [S2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13581617/), [S3](https://www.mdpi.com/2079-7737/15/19/1782).

    **Fix:** Complete the listed supplement/code reads and preserve exact searches and exclusions locally. Audit each precise claim against prior figures, targets, and endpoints. The potentially open contribution is a bounded relationship between measured environmental contrasts and independently evaluated transfer degradation—not heterogeneity alone or familiar lipid/adhesion effects. Failure of S3 to answer that question does not establish that this design can answer it.

12. **[HIGH] Falsification and precision rules remain inadequate.**  
    [g1_question_candidate.md:7](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/reviews/g1_question_candidate.md:7) offers several movable alternatives. “No association” is not a falsifier when intervals are wide. A positive association can coexist with an effect below δ. Choosing δ from attainable precision risks redefining scientific importance to fit available data.

    **Fix:** Define one primary parameter, a scientifically justified practical threshold, disjoint decision rules, and an inconclusive region. Perform a precision-feasibility assessment using four/six environmental model clusters and the actual eligible transfer donors. Seven models do not automatically prohibit every panel-wide association; they do not justify an adaptive gene-class × genotype × context search either.

13. **[MEDIUM] Manifest completeness and reproducibility fall short of “verified provenance.”**  
    [source_manifest_sanger_figshare.json:1](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/data/source_manifest_sanger_figshare.json:1) omits the downloaded Jaccard and VAF files. Neither manifest pins an article version or preserves the authoritative manifest response. Local hash agreement establishes file integrity, not source accuracy. Also, [audit_minipool.py:59](/Users/bmarti44/github/bufferright/research/cancer-dependency-rsi/src/audit_minipool.py:59) does not regenerate the JSON’s direction-check results.

    **Fix:** Manifest every imported source, pin versions/commits, retain source responses and license evidence, and make the audit regenerate its reported outputs. Document the primary-source QC discrepancy: Methods state 500,000 reads, while Extended Data Fig. 9 states five million.

The required narrowing is substantial: **one fixed environmental contrast, one fixed eligible target family, and one panel-wide associational transfer parameter**, conditional on the selected genes and measured models. For example, medium contrasts in dome have four model supports. Remove adaptive context-stratification and causal-attribution promises unless additional evidence supplies identification. Published group-comparison tables could support a separate descriptive concordance question, which needs its own novelty justification.

Before G1 can pass, complete the crosswalk/count/attrition table; resolve preprocessing independence and the actual transfer-data route; correct the heterogeneity interpretation; adjudicate exposure and B reuse; and document an exact, plausibly novel, feasible question. Phase 2 can then finalize the endpoint, meaningful margins, baselines, uncertainty procedure, multiplicity, eligibility, partitions, invalid-result rules, and hashes required for Lock 1. Those later implementation requirements should not be confused with the unresolved G1 evidence requirements.

SCORE: 56
